K15.626-627K. XV 626–627, ch. 3.1 difficulty high (2.78/100; thin 4, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἔστι γὰρ αὐτῶν μία μὲν ἡ κατὰ τὰς 627 χρόας διαφορὰ, ἑτέρα δὲ ἡ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ποιότης καὶ τρίτη κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν καὶ τετάρτη κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν καὶ έ κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν. ἡ μὲν οὖν κατὰ τὴν χρόαν εἰς λευκὸν καὶ μέλανα καὶ κιῤῥὸν καὶ ξανθὸν καὶ ἐρυθρὸν τέμνεται, οὐσῶν μὲν καὶ ἄλλων μεταξὺ διαφορῶν, ἀλλ᾿ ὥσπερ κατὰ τὴν χρόαν, οὕτω καὶ κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν ἐπιμίκτων οὐσῶν. ἡ δὲ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν διαφορὰ τῶν οἴνων εἰς γλυκὺν καὶ αὐστηρὸν τέμνεται, δηλονότι κᾀνταῦθα τὸν ἀμφοῖν μέσον, ὃς οὐδετέραν ἔχει σαφῆ ποιότητα· καὶ πρός γε τούτοις τὸν ἐξ ἀμφοτέρων τῶν ποιοτήτων σύνθετον· ἔτι τε τὸν δριμὺν ὀνομαζόμενον. ἡ δὲ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν διαφορὰ τῶν οἴνων εἴς τε τὸν ὑδατώδη καὶ λεπτὸν καὶ εἰς τὸν πάνυ παχὺν τέμνεται, καὶ δηλονότι καὶ εἰς τὸν μέσον τούτων, εἶτ᾿ αὐθὶς ὅσοι μεταξὺ τοῦ τε μέσου καὶ τῶν ἄκρων εἰσί. καὶ ἡ κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν δὲ διαφορὰ τῶν οἴνων, καθόσον οἱ μὲν εὐώδεις εἰσὶν, οἱ δὲ οὐκ εὐώδεις, ἀλλὰ πληκτικήν τινα καὶ ἀηδῆ τὴν ποιότητα κέκτηνται, τινὲς δὲ οὐδ᾿ ὅλως ἔχουσιν ὀσμὴν, ἀλλ᾿ εἰσὶν ὥσπερ ὕδωρ ἄοδμοι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: έ

For one difference among them is that according to 627 colours, another is the quality according to taste, a third according to consistency, a fourth according to smell, and a fifth according to power. The one according to colour, then, is divided into white, black, tawny, yellow and red, there being other intermediate differences as well, but these being mixed, just as in colour, so also in power. The difference of wines according to taste is divided into sweet and austere, with, obviously, here too the one intermediate between the two, which has neither quality distinctly; and in addition to these the one compounded of both qualities; and further the one called pungent. The difference of wines according to consistency is divided into the watery and thin and into the very thick, and obviously also into the one intermediate between these, and then again all those that lie between the intermediate and the extremes. And the difference of wines according to smell lies in this, that some are fragrant, others not fragrant but possess a certain striking and unpleasant quality, while some have no smell at all, but are odourless like water.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κιῤῥὸν, πληκτικήν, αὐστηρὸν, δριμὺν, έ

For among them there is, first, the difference according to 627 colours; second, the quality according to taste; third, the difference according to consistency; fourth, according to smell; and fifth, according to potency. The difference according to colour is divided into white, black, tawny, yellow and red. There are also other differences between these, but they are mixed, in potency just as in colour. The difference of wines according to taste is divided into sweet and austere. Evidently here too there is the wine intermediate between both, which has neither quality distinctly; in addition to these, the one compounded of both qualities; and further, the one called pungent. The difference of wines according to consistency is divided into the watery and thin and into the very thick. Evidently it is also divided into the one intermediate between these, and then again into those that lie between the intermediate and the extremes. And there is the difference of wines according to smell, insofar as some are fragrant, while others are not fragrant but possess a certain striking and unpleasant quality, and some have no smell at all but are odourless, like water.

K15.627-628K. XV 627–628, ch. 3.1 difficulty low (0.69/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἡ δὲ κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν 628 διαφορὰ τῶν οἴνων ἐστὶ, καθόσον ὁ μὲν ἰσχυρὸς, ὁ δὲ ἀσθενὴς, ὁ δέ τις μέσος, ὁ δέ τις μεταξὺ τοῦ τε μέσου καὶ τῶν ἄκρων ἐστίν· ἰσχυρὸς μὲν ὁ θερμαίνων σφοδρῶς καὶ ταχέως τὸ σῶμα καὶ τὴν κεφαλὴν πληρῶν, ἀσθενὴς δὲ ὁ ἐναντίος· οἱ δ᾿ ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ δῆλοι, τῶν ἄκρων ἀφωρισμένοι. ὀνομάζεται δὲ ὁ μὲν ἰσχυρὸς οἰνώδης, ὁ δ᾿ ἀσθενὴς ὑδατώδης· ᾧ γὰρ ὕδατος οἶνος πλεονεκτεῖ μᾶλλον, τοῦτο ὅταν ἔν τινι θεασώμεθα σφοδρότατον ὑπάρχον, εἰκότως οἰνώδη καλοῦμεν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τοὐναντίον ὑδατώδη τῷ ὕδατι μάλιστα κατὰ πάντα ἐοικότα καὶ τῇ λεπτότητι καὶ τῇ χρόᾳ καὶ τῷ ἀόσμῳ καὶ τῷ μὴ θερμαίνειν ἐπιφανῶς. αὗται μὲν οὖν αἱ κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν φύσιν εἰσὶν ἐν τοῖς οἴνοις διαφοραὶ, προστιθέασι δ᾿ αὐταῖς ἔνιοι τὴν κατὰ τὸν χρόνον, ἐπειδή τινες μὲν ὑπάρχουσιν ἱκανῶς παλαιοὶ, τινὲς δὲ νέοι, τινὲς δὲ ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ τούτων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The difference of wines according to their power 628 consists in the fact that one is strong, another weak, another intermediate, and another lies between the intermediate and the extremes: strong is the one that heats the body vehemently and quickly and fills the head, weak the opposite; and those in between are evident once the extremes have been marked off. The strong one is named vinous, the weak one watery; for that in which wine exceeds water the most—when we observe this to be present in some wine to the highest degree, we reasonably call it vinous, just as, conversely, we call watery the one that most resembles water in all respects, both in thinness and in colour and in being odourless and in not heating perceptibly. These, then, are the differences in wines according to their own proper nature; but some people add to them the difference according to time, since some wines are quite old, some new, and some between these.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀφωρισμένοι, ᾧ γὰρ ὕδατος οἶνος πλεονεκτεῖ μᾶλλον

As to power, 628 the difference of wines is that one is strong, another weak, another intermediate, and another between the intermediate and the extremes. The strong is the one that heats the body vehemently and quickly and fills the head; the weak is its opposite; and those in between are evident, being marked off from the extremes. The strong is called 'vinous', the weak 'watery'. For whenever we observe present in some wine, in the highest degree, that in which wine most exceeds water, we reasonably call it vinous; just as, conversely, we call watery the one that most resembles water in every respect: in thinness, in colour, in lack of smell, and in not heating perceptibly. These, then, are the differences in wines according to their proper nature; but some people add to them the difference according to time, since some wines are quite old, some new, and some in between these.

K15.628-629K. XV 628–629, ch. 3.1 difficulty mid (1.77/100; thin 1, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

εὔδηλον δ᾿ ὅτι τῶν ἔξωθέν εἰσι συμβεβηκότων αἱ τοιαῦται διαφοραί· καθόσον γὰρ ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ δριμύτεροί τε καὶ θερμότεροι γίγνονται, μεταβάλλοντες ἅμα τούτοις καὶ χρόαν καὶ σύστασιν καὶ ὀσμὴν, 629 κατὰ τοσοῦτον ὁ χρόνος διαφοράν τινα ἐν τοῖς οἴνοις ἐργάζεσθαι δοκεῖ· εἰ δέ γε καὶ μηδὲν τούτων ὑπήλλαττεν, οὐδ᾿ ἂν ἐλέγετο πρός τινων παλαιὸς ὁ οἶνος ἢ νέος· ἀλλ᾿ οὐδὲ νῦν οὕτω λέγουσιν, ὡς τῶν ἐτῶν αὐτοῦ δεόμενοι κατὰ πρῶτον λόγον, ἀλλ᾿ ὡς ἄμα τοῖς ἔτεσιν ἐνδεικνύμενοι τὴν δύναμιν· οὐ γὰρ διὰ τὸν χρόνον οἱ οἶνοι τὰς ὠφελείας καὶ βλάβας ἔχουσιν, ἀλλ᾿ ἐκ τῆς τῶν οἰκείων διαφορῶν τούτων᾿ ὑπαλλαγῆς. κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς οὖν τοῦ χρόνου μνημονεύομεν ἐν ταῖς τῶν οἴνων διαφοραῖς, οὐ κυρίως οὐδὲ πρώτως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τούτων᾿

It is quite clear that differences of this kind belong to the things that are accidents from outside; for insofar as in the course of time wines become more pungent and hotter, changing along with these also their colour, consistency and smell, 629 to that extent time seems to produce a certain difference in wines; but if it altered none of these things, wine would not be called by anyone old or new; yet even now people do not speak in this way as though they required its years in the primary sense, but as indicating its power together with the years; for it is not on account of time that wines have their benefits and harms, but from the alteration of these proper differences of theirs. Accidentally, therefore, do we mention time among the differences of wines, not in the strict sense nor primarily.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τούτων᾿, δεόμενοι, τῶν ἔξωθέν εἰσι συμβεβηκότων

It is quite clear that differences of this kind belong to the external accidents. For inasmuch as in the course of time wines become sharper and hotter, changing together with these qualities their colour, consistency and smell as well, 629 to that extent time seems to produce some difference in wines. But if it altered none of these things, the wine would not be called old or new by anyone. Indeed, not even now do people speak in this way as having need of its years in the primary sense, but as indicating, together with the years, its power. For it is not on account of time that wines have their benefits and harms, but from the alteration of these proper differences of theirs. It is therefore accidentally that we mention time among the differences of wines, not properly, nor primarily.

K15.629-630K. XV 629–630, ch. 3.1, 3.2 difficulty high (2.94/100; thin 3, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ αὐτῶν μέντοι τῶν οἰκείων ἐν αὐτοῖς διαφορῶν, αἱ μὲν εἰς ὠφέλειάν τε καὶ βλάβην ἀναφέρονται κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον, αἱ δὲ οὐ κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον, ἀλλὰ κατὰ συμβεβηκός· γλυκύτης μὲν καὶ αὐστηρότης κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον, οὕτω δὲ καὶ ἡ ἰσχὺς καὶ ἀσθένεια καὶ λεπτότης καὶ πάχος· αἱ δὲ κατὰ τὴν χρόαν τε καὶ ὀσμὴν οὐ πρώτως, ἀλλὰ κατὰ συμβεβηκός· ἐπεὶ τῶν οἴνων οἱ μὲν ὑδατώδεις τέ εἰσι καὶ λευκοὶ καὶ κιῤῥοὶ καὶ μέλανες, οἱ δὲ ὑδατώδεις καὶ ἄοδμοι 630 ὥσπερ ὕδωρ, ἢ εὐώδεις ὅσπερ ὥρωμα καὶ μύρον. εἴπερ οὖν τῶν οἴνων αὗταί εἰσιν αἱ διαφοραὶ καὶ κατά τε τὴν οἰκείαν δύναμιν ἀριθμούμεναι καὶ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, εὔδηλον ἤδη σοι περὶ τῆς ἐν τῇ προκειμένῃ ῥήσει κατά τε τὴν τάξιν αὐτῶν καὶ τὸν ἀριθμὸν ἐνδείας· ἀλλ᾿ ἐὰν φαίνηται κατὰ τὸν ἑξῆς λόγον, ἁπασῶν τῶν χρησίμων διαφορῶν μνημονεύων ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, συγχωρήσομεν αὐτοῦ τῇ κατὰ τὴν ἑρμηνείαν ἐνδείᾳ. Ὁ μὲν γλυκὺς ἧσσόν ἐστι καρηβαρικώτερος τοῦ οἰνώδεος καὶ ἧσσον φρενῶν ἁπτόμενος καὶ διαχωρητικώτερος δή τι τοῦ ἑτέρου κατ᾿ ἔντερον· μεγαλόσπλαγχνος δὲ σπληνός καὶ ἥπατος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑδατώδεις (first occurrence, in the list of colours), ὥρωμα, ὅσπερ, ἧσσόν ἐστι καρηβαρικώτερος

And indeed, of the proper differences in them themselves, some are referred to benefit and harm in their own right, others not in their own right but accidentally: sweetness and austerity in their own right, and likewise also strength and weakness and thinness and thickness; whereas the differences in colour and odour are not primary but accidental, since among wines some are watery-coloured and white and tawny and dark, while others are watery and odourless 630 like water, or fragrant like an aromatic (ὥρωμα) and perfume. If, then, these are the differences of wines, both those enumerated according to their proper power and those according to accident, it is already quite clear to you concerning the deficiency in the passage before us, with respect both to their order and to their number; but if, in the discourse that follows, Hippocrates appears to make mention of all the useful differences, we shall pardon him the deficiency in his expression. The sweet wine is less heavy on the head than the vinous one and less apt to affect the phrenes, and somewhat more purgative than the other by way of the intestine; but it is productive of enlarged viscera, of the spleen and of the liver.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὥρωμα (presumably for ἄρωμα, 'spice'), ὅσπερ (presumably for ὥσπερ), καρηβαρικώτερος (comparative with ἧσσον, redundant; perhaps for καρηβαρικός), φρενῶν ἁπτόμενος ('affect the mind' vs. 'diaphragm'), μεγαλόσπλαγχνος

And indeed, of the differences proper to wines themselves, some are referred to benefit and harm according to their own proper account, others not according to their own account but incidentally. Sweetness and austerity are referred according to their own account, and likewise strength and weakness, and thinness and thickness. Those concerning colour and smell are not referred primarily but incidentally, since some wines are watery, white, tawny and black, while others are watery and odourless 630 like water, or fragrant like a spice and an unguent. If, then, these are the differences of wines, counted both according to their proper power and incidentally, the deficiency in the passage before us, as regards both their order and their number, is now quite clear to you. But if Hippocrates appears in the subsequent discourse to mention all the useful differences, we shall pardon his deficiency of expression. The sweet wine is less productive of heaviness of the head than the vinous, less apt to affect the mind, and somewhat more laxative through the bowel than the other; but it causes enlargement of the viscera, of the spleen and the liver.

Greek (Kühn)

οὐκ ἐπιτήδειος οὖν οὐδὲ τοῖσι πικροχόλοισι· καὶ γὰρ οὖν διψώδης τοῖσί γε τοιουτέοισίν ἐστιν· ἀτὰρ καὶ φυσώδης ἐντέρου τοῦ ἄνω, οὐ μὴν πολέμιός γε αὐτῷ τῷ κάτω ἐντέρῳ ὡς κατὰ λόγον τῆς φύσης, καίτοι γε 631 οὐ πάνυ πορίμη ἐστὶν ἡ ἀπὸ τοῦ γλυκέος οἴνου φύση, ἀλλ᾿ ἐγχρονίζει περὶ ὑποχόνδρια. Κατὰ τὴν γευστικὴν ποιότητα γλυκὺς οἶνος ὀνομάζεται, καθ᾿ ἣν καὐστηρός ἐστι καὶ δριμὺς, ἵνα τὸν ὑδατώδη παραλίπωμεν, οὐκ ἐκπεπτωκότα τελείως τοῦ στύφειν, εἰ καὶ παντάπασιν ἐκλελυμένην ἔχει τὴν ποιότητα τῆς στύψεως. εἰ γὰρ ὕδατι παραβάλλοις αὐτὸν, αἰσθήσῃ σαφῶς ἐκ τῆς παραβολῆς τὴν γεῦσιν τῶν τοιούτων οἴνων γεγονέναι βραχεῖαν στύψιν εἰληφότων. ὑδατώδη δὲ καλοῦσιν οἶνον οἱ ἄνθρωποι τὸν ἐοικότα κατά τε χρόαν καὶ σύστασιν τῷ ὕδατι, διαφανής τε γὰρ ὡς ἐκεῖνο καὶ λαμπρὸς καὶ καθαρὸς καὶ τῇ συστάσει λεπτὸς φαίνεται· εὐθὺς δὲ καὶ τῇ δυνάμει παραπλήσιός ἐστιν ὁ τοιοῦτος ὕδατι, μήτε κεφαλῆς ἀσθενοῦς ἁπτόμενος μήτε νεύρων ἀσθενῶν, ὅτι μηδὲ θερμαίνει σαφῶς. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τούτου καὶ αὖθις εἰρήσεται.

It is therefore not suitable either for the bitter-bilious; for indeed it is thirst-producing for such people; moreover it is also flatulent for the upper bowel, though not hostile to the lower bowel itself in proportion to the flatulence, and yet 631 the flatulence arising from sweet wine is not very easily passed, but lingers about the hypochondria. Wine is called sweet in respect of its gustatory quality, in respect of which it is also austere and pungent—to leave aside the watery wine, which has not entirely fallen away from being astringent, even if it has the quality of astringency altogether slackened. For if you compare it with water, you will perceive clearly from the comparison that the taste of such wines is that of wines that have acquired a slight astringency. People call watery the wine that resembles water in both colour and consistency; for it appears transparent like water, and bright and clear and thin in consistency; and such a wine is at once also similar to water in its power, affecting neither a weak head nor weak nerves, because it does not heat perceptibly either. But about this more will be said again later.

It is therefore not suitable for the bitter-bilious either; for indeed it causes thirst in such people at least. Moreover, it is also productive of flatus in the upper intestine, yet it is not hostile to the lower intestine itself, as might be expected in proportion to the flatus; although 631 the flatus from sweet wine is not very easily passed, but lingers about the hypochondria. Wine is called sweet according to the gustatory quality, the same quality according to which a wine is also austere and acrid, to pass over the watery wine, which has not entirely lost the property of astringing, even if it has the quality of astringency altogether relaxed. For if you compare it with water, you will clearly perceive from the comparison that your taste of such wines has been of wines that have taken on a slight astringency. Watery wine is the name people give to the wine that resembles water both in colour and in consistency; for it appears transparent like water, and bright and pure, and thin in consistency. And it follows directly that such a wine is also close to water in its power, affecting neither a weak head nor weak nerves, because it does not even heat perceptibly. But about this it will be said again later.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Θήρινός, Καρυΐνος, Σκυβελίτης

Sweet wine, however, has been so named from its quality in respect of taste, but it is separated from water by a very great distance in 632 colour and consistency; for you will never see a sweet wine that is exactly thin or white, but rather one standing more or less apart from the characteristics mentioned, yet possessing neither of them. At any rate the Theraean and the Scybelite are sweet wines to an outstanding degree, both being thick and black; sweet too are those produced in Maeonia, the Therinos and the Karyinos, which differ from those mentioned in thickness and blackness to the same degree as they fall short of them in sweetness; and moreover what is called hepsēma and siraion, which is produced from must being boiled down, in proportion as it is rendered sweeter than wine, appears to that same extent both blacker in colour and thicker in consistency. It has been shown, then, also in the fourth book On the Powers of Simple Drugs that every sweet humour comes into being through heat, not however heat that is immoderate and vehement, as the acrid humour does in things that heat vehemently, for acridity is inseparable from these; but in actual use sweet wine is seen to heat moderately, and its thirst-provoking character belongs to it both on account of its heat and on account of its thickness.

Sweet wine is so named from its quality as regards taste, but it is separated from water by a very great distance in 632 colour and consistency. For you will never see a sweet wine that is exactly thin or white; rather, you will see one standing more or less removed from the qualities just mentioned, but possessing neither of them. At any rate, sweet wines par excellence are the Theraean and the Scybelites, both of them thick and black. Sweet also are those produced in Maeonia, the Therinus and the Caryinus, which differ from those mentioned in thickness and blackness by as much as they fall short of them in sweetness. And indeed what is called hepsema and siraion, made from must that is boiled, appears blacker in colour and thicker in consistency by as much as it is rendered sweeter than wine. Now it has been shown in the fourth book On the Power of Simple Drugs that every sweet flavour comes about through heat, though not heat that is immoderate and violent, as the pungent flavour does in things that heat violently, for pungency is inseparable from these. Rather, in use sweet wine appears to heat moderately, and its thirst-producing character belongs to it on account of both its heat and its thickness.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπεροπτηθὲν

Thus, at any rate, must too, to the extent that it has contact with the fire for a longer time, 633 becomes thicker, blacker and sweeter; what has been over-cooked, however, changes into bitterness, just as honey too, although it is the sweetest of all things, nevertheless also takes on bitterness upon very prolonged boiling. It has been stated, as I said, in a single book, the fourth of On the Powers of Simple Drugs, concerning all the gustatory qualities, and now, as in the other exegeses of the books of Hippocrates, I state not the demonstrations but only their conclusions. Since, then, sweet wine heats the viscus, and is also, as has been said, thick in consistency, its particular powers, which it displays when drunk, follow upon the secondary qualities; for colour, in its own right, has no power for benefit or harm.

εἰς ὅσον οὖν παχύς τ᾿ ἐστὶ κατὰ σύστασιν ὁ τοιοῦτος οἶνος, τοσοῦτον καὶ βραδύπορος, ὥστ᾿ οὐ μόνον οὐκ ἐκφράξει τι τῶν ἐμπεφραγμένων μορίων, ἀλλὰ καὶ προσεμφράξει· καὶ διὰ τοῦτο βλαβερώτατος ἥπατι πάσχοντι, καὶ μάλισθ᾿, ὅταν ἤτοι φλεγμονή τις ἢ σκίῤῥος ᾖ 634 κατὰ τοῦτο· μετριωτέραν δ᾿ ἐργάζεται τὴν βλάβην, ὅταν ὑπὸ παχέων χυμῶν ἐμφράττηται τὸ σπλάγχνον ἢ δι᾿ ἀτονίαν πάσχῃ, καθάπερ ἐν τοῖς ἰδίως ἡπατικοῖς ὀνομαζομένοις πάθεσιν. ἐφεξῆς δὲ ἥπατι βλάπτεται σπλὴν ὑπὸ παχέων οἴνων, οὐ μὴν ὅ γε πνεύμων, ὅταν ἐν τοῖς βρογχίοις ἔχῃ παχὺν χυμόν. κατὰ μὲν γὰρ τὸ ἧπαρ ἐξ εὐρέων ἀγγείων τῶν ἐπὶ ταῖς πύλαις εἰς στενὰ παραγίνεται πέρατα τῶν ἐν τοῖς σιμοῖς αὐτοῦ μέρεσι καὶ τῶν ἐν τοῖς κυρτοῖς, καθ᾿ ἃ καὶ ἡ μετάληψις ἁπάντων τῶν ἀναδιδομένων εἰς ὅλον τὸ σῶμα γίγνεται· κατὰ δὲ τὸν πνεύμονα τοὐναντίον συμβαίνει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

To the extent, then, that such a wine is thick in consistency, to that extent it is also slow in passing, so that not only will it not clear any of the parts that have become obstructed, but it will even add further obstruction; and for this reason it is most harmful to a liver that is affected, and especially whenever there is either some inflammation or a scirrhus 634 in it; but it produces a more moderate harm whenever the viscus is obstructed by thick humours or is affected through atony, as in the affections properly called hepatic. Next after the liver, the spleen is harmed by thick wines, but not the lung, whenever it has thick humour in the bronchia. For in the liver, [the wine] passes from the wide vessels at the portals into the narrow extremities of those in its concave parts and of those in its convex parts, at which also the transference of everything that is distributed into the whole body takes place; but in the lung the opposite happens.

διὰ πολλῶν γὰρ φλεβῶν ἀναδοθεὶς καὶ προκατεργασθεὶς ἐν αὐταῖς, ἐμπίπτει ταῖς εὐρυχωρίαις τῶν βρογχίων, προτέραις μὲν ταῖς τῶν μικροτέρων, ἐξ ἐκείνων δ᾿ εἰς τὰ μείζονα παραλαμβάνεται, συναναφέρων ἑαυτῷ καὶ τοὺς παχεῖς χυμοὺς, ὧν οὐ μόνον θερμότερος, ἀλλὰ καὶ λεπτότερός ἐστιν· ἔμαθες δ᾿ ὅτι ὑπὸ τῶν βηχῶν ὁ πνεύμων ἐκκαθαίρεται τοὺς παχεῖς χυμοὺς καὶ γλίσχρους· ὅσοι γὰρ ὑδατώδεις εἰσὶ καὶ ἀκριβῶς λεπτοὶ, περιῤῥέουσι τῷ κατὰ τὰς βῆχας ἐκπεμπομένῳ τῷ πνεύματι· τοῦτο γὰρ οἱονεὶ 635 χείρ τίς ἐστιν ἀναφέρουσα τοὺς παχεῖς χυμοὺς ἅμ᾿ αὐτῷ· προσήκει δὲ δηλονότι τὸ πάχος τῶν χυμῶν εἶναι τοιοῦτον, ὡς ὑπὸ τοῦ πνεύματος ἄνω φέρεσθαι δύνασθαι καὶ μὴ καθάπερ πηλὸν ἐμπεπλάσθαι τοῖς βρογχίοις. διὰ τοῦτό γε καὶ τοὺς γλίσχρους χυμοὺς αἱ βῆχες οὐκ ἐκκαθαίρουσι· καὶ γὰρ μένουσι καὶ ἔνδον ἔχονται τῶν βρογχίων ἰξοῦ δίκην. ὑγρότητος οὖν συμμέτρου δεῖ τοῖς μέλλουσι φαρμάκοις εἰς τὴν ἀναγωγὴν τῶν ἐν πνεύμονι περιεχομένων παχέων χυμῶν ἐπιτηδείοις ἔσεσθαι· γλίσχρων δ᾿ ὄντων αὐτῶν οὐχ ὑγρότητος μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥύψεως ἐστι χρεία, γιγνομένης ὑπὸ τῶν ὀξέων χυμῶν. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μελίκρατον μὲν ἐπιτηδειότατόν ἐστι τοῖς παχέσι χυμοῖς εἰς ἀνάπτυσιν, ὀξύμελι δὲ τοῖς γλίσχροις· ἐφεξῆς δὲ τῷ μελικράτῳ πτισάνη· καὶ μετὰ ταύτην ὁ γλυκὺς οἶνος, ἤδη πεπεμμένης τῆς περιπνευμονίας τε καὶ πλευρίτιδος πινόμενος, οὐ φλεγμαινόντων ἔτι τῶν σωμάτων· εἰ δὲ διψώδης εἴη πινόμενος ὁ τοιοῦτος οἶνος, ἧττον ἐπιτήδειος εἰς τὴν ἀνάπτυσιν γίνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥύψεως

For, having been distributed through many veins and worked up beforehand in them, it falls into the wide spaces of the bronchi, first into those of the smaller ones, and from those it is taken up into the larger, carrying up along with itself also the thick humours, than which it is not only warmer but also thinner. And you have learned that the lung is cleared of the thick and viscous humours by coughs; for those humours which are watery and perfectly thin flow around the breath that is sent out in the coughs; for this breath is, as it were, 635 a kind of hand that carries up the thick humours together with itself. But clearly the thickness of the humours ought to be such that they can be borne upward by the breath and are not plastered, like mud, onto the bronchi. For this very reason coughs do not clear out the viscous humours either; for they remain and cling within the bronchi in the manner of birdlime. Hence a moderate moisture is needed in the drugs that are going to be suitable for the bringing up of the thick humours contained in the lung; but when these are viscous there is need not only of moisture but also of a cleansing action, which is brought about by sharp humours. And for this reason melikraton is most suitable for the thick humours with a view to expectoration, and oxymel for the viscous ones; next after melikraton comes ptisan; and after this, sweet wine, drunk when the peripneumonia and pleuritis have already been concocted and the bodies are no longer inflamed; but if such a wine, when drunk, should be thirst-provoking, it becomes less suitable for expectoration.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: περιῤῥέουσι, ῥύψεως, ὀξέων χυμῶν, διψώδης

For, having been distributed through many veins and worked up beforehand in them, it falls into the cavities of the bronchia, first into those of the smaller ones, and from those it is taken on into the larger, carrying up along with itself the thick humours as well, than which it is not only hotter but also thinner. You have learned that the lung is cleansed of thick and viscous humours by coughs; for those humours that are watery and exactly thin flow around the breath that is sent out in coughing, since this breath is, as it were, 635 a hand that carries the thick humours up along with itself. Clearly the thickness of the humours must be such that they can be carried upward by the breath, and are not plastered onto the bronchia like mud. For this reason coughs do not cleanse out the viscous humours either, for these remain and cling within the bronchia like birdlime. Therefore a moderate moistness is required in drugs that are to be suitable for bringing up the thick humours contained in the lung; but when these humours are viscous, there is need not only of moistness but also of detersion, which is produced by sour juices. And for this reason melicrat is most suitable for the expectoration of thick humours, and oxymel for that of viscous ones. Next after melicrat comes ptisane; and after this sweet wine, drunk when the peripneumonia and pleuritis have already been concocted and the bodies are no longer inflamed. But if such wine should be thirst-provoking when drunk, it becomes less suitable for expectoration.

K15.635-636K. XV 635–636, ch. 3.2 difficulty mid (1.29/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

συνελόντως δὲ φάναι, γλυκὺς οἶνος ἐν ὀξεῖ νοσήματι εἰς ἀνάπτυσιν μὲν 636 ἐπιτήδειός ἐστι, κατά γε τὰς εἰρημένας ἐνεργείας αὐτοῦ καὶ κατὰ τὸν κοινὸν ἁπάντων οἴνων λόγον ἐκ τοῦ ῥωννύναι τὴν δύναμιν· μάλιστα δὲ τοῖς ἀναπτύουσι τῶν ἐν τῷ θώρακι καὶ πνεύμονι περιεχομένων ὑγρῶν ὁτιοῦν, (ἔμαθες γὰρ αὐτὰ ποικίλα ταῖς συστάσεσι καὶ ταῖς. ποιότησι καὶ ταῖς δυνάμεσιν ὄντα κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν βιβλίον, ἔνθα τὰς τῶν πτυσμάτων ἐδήλου διαφορὰς) ἄλλο δ᾿ οὐδὲν ἀγαθὸν ὁ τοιοῦτος οἶνος ἔχει πυρέττουσι διδόμενος, ἀλλ᾿ ἧττον μὲν τοῦ σφοδρῶς θερμαίνοντος οἰνώδους βλάπτειν δυνήσεται, χρηστὸν δ᾿ οὐδὲν ἄλλο παρὰ τὰ προειρημένα, ὅτι μὴ συλλαμβάνει τι βραχὺ τῇ διεξόδῳ τῶν κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα. διὰ τοῦτο οὖν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, ἀφ᾿ ὧν ἧττον βλάπτει τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆς διδασκαλίας ποιησάμενος, ὄντων πολλῶν, ἐπὶ τὰς ἰδίας ὠφελείας αὐτῶν μετέβη κατὰ τὴν τοῦ λόγου τελευτὴν, ὀλίγας οὔσας. ἐπέλθωμεν οὖν ἃ λέγει, ταῖς λέξεσιν ἀκολουθήσαντες αὐτοῦ· ἧττον αὐτὸν ἅπτεσθαι φρενῶν φησιν ἢ οἱ ἰσχυροὶ τῶν οἴνων, τουτέστιν ἧττον ἐργάζεσθαι παραφροσύνας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φρενῶν

To put it concisely, sweet wine in an acute disease is suitable for expectoration, both in virtue of the activities of it that have been mentioned and in virtue of the account common to all wines, that of strengthening the faculty; and it is suitable most of all for those who are bringing up by expectoration any of the humours whatsoever contained in the thorax and lung (for you learned that these are varied in their consistencies, their qualities and their powers, in the book Prognostic, where he set out the differences of the sputa). But such a wine has no other good when given to those with fever; rather, it will be able to do less harm than the vinous wine that heats violently, yet it has nothing else beneficial beyond what has been stated, except that it contributes some small assistance to the passage through of the contents of the belly. For this reason, then, Hippocrates, having made the beginning of his teaching from the respects in which it does less harm, these being many, passed over at the end of the discussion to its own particular benefits, which are few. Let us therefore go through what he says, following his own words. He says that it lays hold of the phrenes less than the strong wines do, that is, that it produces derangements of mind less.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταῖς. ποιότησι (stray punctuation in Kühn's text), οἰνώδους (the 'vinous' type of wine), ἅπτεσθαι φρενῶν, ἐκ τοῦ ῥωννύναι τὴν δύναμιν

To put it in summary: sweet wine in an acute disease is suitable 636 for expectoration, at least in respect of its actions already described, and in respect of the principle common to all wines, namely that it strengthens the vital strength (δύναμις); and it is suitable above all for those who are expectorating any of the fluids contained in the thorax and lung (for you have learned that these are varied in their consistencies, qualities and powers, from the book Prognostic, where he set out the differences of the sputa). But such wine has no other good when given to those who are febrile: it will indeed be able to do less harm than the vinous wine that heats strongly, but it has nothing else beneficial beyond what has been said, except that it contributes a little to the passage of the contents of the belly. For this reason, then, Hippocrates, having begun his teaching from the respects in which it does less harm, which are many, passed over at the end of the discourse to its particular benefits, which are few. Let us therefore go through what he says, following his wording. He says that it affects the wits (φρενῶν) less than the strong wines do, that is, it produces derangements (παραφροσύνας) less.

Greek (Kühn)

οἱ γὰρ ἰσχυροὶ σφοδρῶς θερμαίνοντες ἐμπιπλᾶσι 637 τὴν κεφαλὴν καὶ χυμῶν καὶ ἀτμῶν καὶ θερμασίας. εἰς ὅσον οὖν ἀπολείπονται σφοδρᾶς θερμότητος οἱ γλυκεῖς, εἰς τοσοῦτον ἧττόν τε καὶ καρηβαρίαν ἐργάζονται καὶ βλάβην τῆς φρονήσεως. διαχωρητικωτέρους δὲ αὐτοὺς εἶναί φησι τῶν ἄλλων καὶ μεγαλοσπλάγχνους, τουτέστιν αὐξάνοντας τῶν σπλάγχνων τοὺς ὄγκους. εἴρηταί μοι περὶ τούτων ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν. οὐκ ἐπιτήδειόν γε μὴν τὸν γλυκὺν οἶνον οὐδὲ τοῖς πικροχόλοις εἶναί φησι, τουτέστι τοῖς τὴν πικρὰν χολὴν ἀθροίζουσιν, εἴθισται γὰρ τοῖς ἰατροῖς χολὴν μὲν ἁπλῶς ὀνομάζειν τὴν ὠχράν τε καὶ ξανθὴν, τὴν μέλαιναν δὲ χολὴν, ὅλον τοῦτο λέγειν, οὐχ ἁπλῶς χολήν· ἀλλ᾿ αὕτη μὲν ὀξεῖα, πικρὰ δ᾿ ἡ ξανθὴ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τοὺς πικροχόλους Ἱπποκράτης ὀνομάζει τοὺς πλεονάζουσαν ἔχοντας τὴν τοιαύτην χολήν. ἐπιμιγνυμένη μὲν οὖν ὑδατώδεσιν ἰχῶρσιν ὑγροτάτη τε καὶ ὠχρὰ φαίνεται· μόνη δ᾿ οὖσα, παχεῖα καὶ ξανθή.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For the strong ones, heating vehemently, fill 637 the head with humours and vapours and heat. To the extent, then, that the sweet ones fall short of vehement heat, to that extent they produce less both heaviness of the head and damage to the understanding. He says that they are more laxative than the others and "enlargers of the viscera" (megalosplanchnoi), that is, increasing the bulk of the viscera. I have spoken about these matters a little earlier. He says moreover that sweet wine is not suitable even for the bitter-bilious, that is, for those who accumulate bitter bile; for physicians are accustomed to call the pale and yellow bile simply "bile", but black bile they call by this whole expression, not simply "bile"; and the latter is acid, whereas the yellow is bitter, and for this reason Hippocrates names "bitter-bilious" those who have such bile in excess. When mixed with watery ichors, then, it appears very fluid and pale; but when it is by itself, thick and yellow.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅλον τοῦτο λέγειν, μεγαλοσπλάγχνους, ὠχράν

For the strong wines, heating vehemently, fill 637 the head with humours, vapours and heat. To the extent, then, that the sweet wines fall short of vehement heat, to that extent they produce less both heaviness of the head and harm to the understanding. He says that they are more apt to pass through the bowels than the others, and 'viscera-enlarging' (megalosplanchnous), that is, increasing the bulk of the viscera. I have spoken about these matters a little earlier. Yet he says that sweet wine is not suitable for the bitter-bilious either, that is, for those who accumulate bitter bile. For physicians are accustomed to call 'bile' without qualification the pale and the yellow, but in the case of black bile to say this whole expression, not simply 'bile'. But this latter is sharp, whereas the yellow is bitter, and for this reason Hippocrates calls 'bitter-bilious' those who have such bile in excess. When it is mixed with watery ichors it appears very fluid and pale; but when it is on its own, thick and yellow.

K15.637-638K. XV 637–638, ch. 3.2 difficulty high (2.98/100; thin 3, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

θερμαὶ δὲ καὶ ξηραὶ κράσεις εἰσὶν αἱ γεννῶσαι τὴν τοιαύτην χολὴν, ἐπιτήδειοί γε μὴν εἰς γένεσιν αὐτῆς εἰσι πάντες οἱ γλυκεῖς χυμοί· διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὸ μέλι πάντων 638 αὐτῶν ἐκχολοῦται μάλιστα, διότι γλυκύτατον· εἶθ᾿ ἑξῆς οἱ γλυκεῖς οἶνοι. οὐ μόνον δ᾿ ὅτι ἡ πολλὴ θερμασία τοὺς τοιούτους χυμοὺς ἐργάζεται πικροὺς, ἀνεπιτήδειός ἐστιν ὁ γλυκὺς οἶνος τοῖς πυρέττουσι, καὶ μάλισθ᾿ ὅταν ὦσι θερμοὶ καὶ ξηροὶ τὴν κρᾶσιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ διότι παχεῖς ὄντες οὔτ᾿ ἐπ᾿ οὖρα διεξέρχονται ταχέως οὔτε διαπνέονται ταχέως, ὥστ᾿ οὐ συνάγουσιν ἑαυτοῖς οὐδὲ συνεκκενοῦσι τοὺς χολώδεις χυμούς. ἓν οὖν αὐτοῖς τούτοις μόνοις ἀγαθὸν ὑπάρχει τὸ τὴν γαστέρα λαπάττειν· ὥστ᾿ ἄν γε μὴ ποιήσωσι τοῦτο, παντοίως βλάπτουσι. πρὸς γὰρ τούτοις καὶ διψώδεις εἰσὶν, αὐτὸ τοῦτο δηλοῦντες, ὡς ἐκχολοῦνται ῥᾳδίως· λέγει δὲ τὸν τοιοῦτον εἶναι καὶ φυσώδη· θερμαινόμενοι γὰρ οἱ παχεῖς χυμοὶ πνεῦμα γεννῶσιν ἑαυτοῖς ὅμοτον ἀτμῶδές τε καὶ παχύ· τοιοῦτον δέ ἐστι τὸ τὰς πνευματώσεις ἐργαζόμενον, ὡς ἔκ γε τῶν λεπτῶν οἴνων οὔτε γεννᾶται πνεῦμα, κᾂν γεννηθῇ δέ ποτε, λεπτομερές τε καὶ ἀερῶδες, οὐχ ὁμιχλῶδές τε καὶ ἀτμῶδες.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅμοτον, αὐτοῖς τούτοις μόνοις, πνευματώσεις

The temperaments that generate this kind of bile are hot and dry; yet all sweet humours are suited to its generation. For this reason honey, too, of all 638 of them turns most into bile, because it is sweetest; and next after it the sweet wines. And sweet wine is unsuitable for those with fever, and especially when they are hot and dry in temperament, not only because abundant heat makes such humours bitter, but also because, being thick, they neither pass out quickly by way of the urines nor are transpired quickly, so that they do not gather up the bilious humours along with themselves nor evacuate them together with themselves. One good thing alone, then, belongs to these very wines, namely that they relax the belly; so that if they do not do this, they do harm in every way. For in addition to these things they are also thirst-provoking, showing by this very fact that they readily turn into bile; and he says that such a wine is also flatulent; for the thick humours, when heated, generate a pneuma like themselves, vaporous and thick; and such is the pneuma that produces flatulent distensions, whereas from thin wines, at any rate, pneuma is not generated, and even if it is at some time generated, it is fine-particled and airy, not misty and vaporous.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅμοτον (read as ὅμοιον, 'like'), χυμοί (juices vs. humours/flavours), συνάγουσιν, πνευματώσεις

It is hot and dry mixtures that generate bile of this kind; and all sweet juices are, indeed, suited to its generation. For this reason honey too, of all of 638 them, is turned into bile most of all, because it is the sweetest; and next after it the sweet wines. Sweet wine is unsuitable for those who are feverish, and especially when they are hot and dry in their mixture, not only because great heat makes such juices bitter, but also because, being thick, they neither pass through quickly to the urine nor are quickly dispersed by transpiration, so that they do not draw together with themselves, nor evacuate along with themselves, the bilious humours. These alone, then, have one good: loosening the belly; so that if they do not do this, they do harm in every way. For besides this they are also thirst-provoking, which shows this very thing, that they are readily turned into bile. He says that such wine is also flatulent; for thick juices, when heated, generate a pneuma like themselves, vaporous and thick, and it is such pneuma that produces inflations. Whereas from thin wines pneuma is not generated, or, if it is ever generated, it is fine-particled and airy, not misty and vaporous.

But he says that sweet wine is not harmful to the lower intestines, even though 639 the flatulence arising from it is slow in passing and lingers about the hypochondrium. And indeed this very thing is the reason why it does not greatly trouble the lower parts of the intestines: that it lingers about the hypochondrium until it has been thoroughly concocted and completely thinned; and once it has been concocted, it comes to have a nature too that is both more readily passable and finer-parted, so that, on the same principle by which it was thrust downward from the upper parts, on the same principle it is sent forth outward by these lower parts. "For indeed this wine is, on the whole, less diuretic than the vinous white." Let us consider which wine he has called "vinous white".

He says that sweet wine is not harmful to the lower intestines, even though 639 the flatus from it is slow to pass and lingers about the hypochondrium. Greek (Kühn)

λέλεκται γὰρ ἔμπροσθεν οἰνώδης οἶνος εἶναί τε καὶ λέγεσθαι προσηκόντως ὁ τοῖς ὑδατώδεσιν ἀντικείμενος· ἰσχυρῶς οὖν αὐτὸν δεῖ θερμαίνειν καὶ κεφαλὴν πληροῦν καὶ παραφροσύνας ἐργάζεσθαι καὶ τὴν πυρετώδη θερμασίαν αὐξάνειν· ἀλλ᾿ ὁ 640 τοιοῦτος οἶνος οὐκ ἔστι λευκὸς, οὔτε νέος ὢν οὔτε παλαιωθεὶς, ὅτι μηδὲ ἄλλος τις τῶν παλαιουμένων οἴνων διαμένει λευκὸς, κᾂν ὅτι μάλιστα κατ᾿ ἀρχὰς ὑπάρξῃ τοιοῦτος, ἀλλ᾿ ἀεὶ καὶ μᾶλλον ἑαυτοῦ κιῤῥότερος γίγνεται, τελευταῖον δὲ καὶ ξανθός. ἔοικεν οὖν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης εἰρηκέναι λευκὸν οἰνώδη τὸν αὐστηρὸν, ὃς οὐδ᾿ αὐτός ἐστιν οὐρητικὸς, ἀλλ᾿ ἐὰν μὲν παχὺς ᾖ ὁ τοιοῦτος, χρονίζει περὶ ὑποχόνδριον, ἐὰν δὲ μὴ παχὺς, οὐδὲ χρονίζει μέχρι πολλοῦ, καθάπερ ὁ μέλας, οὔθ᾿ ὁμοίως ἐπ᾿ οὖρα διεξέρχεται τοῖς λευκοῖς τε ἅμα καὶ μετρίως αὐστηροῖς· οὐρητικοὶ γὰρ οὗτοι διότι πεφεύγασιν ἀμφοτέρας τὰς ἰδέας τοῦ βραδέως ἐπ᾿ οὖρα διεξέρχεσθαι, τήν τε τοῦ ὑδατώδους, ἣν διὰ ψυχρότητα πέπονθε, καὶ τὴν τοῦ μέλανος οἴνου διὰ τὸ πάχος. ὁ γὰρ λευκὸς οἶνος καὶ αὐστηρὸς ἥκιστα θερμὸς ὡς ἐν οἴνοις ἐστὶ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τοῖς κατὰ γαστέρα ῥεύμασιν ἐπιτήδειος, οὐ μὴν οὐρητικός γε ἱκανῶς ὁ τοιοῦτός ἐστιν· ἴσως οὖν τούτῳ παραβάλλων τὸν μέλανά φησιν ἧττον οὐρητικὸν ὑπάρχειν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μέλανα (in the final sentence, where the lemma concerns the sweet wine)

For it was said earlier that the wine which is opposed to the watery wines both is and is properly called "vinous" wine; such a wine, then, must heat strongly, fill the head, produce deliria, and increase the febrile heat. But such a 640 wine is not white, neither when it is new nor when it has aged, since no other of the wines that are being aged remains white either, even if at the beginning it is such to the highest degree, but it always becomes ever more tawny than it was, and finally even yellow. So Hippocrates seems to have called "white vinous" the austere wine, which is not itself diuretic either; rather, if such a wine is thick, it lingers around the hypochondrium, while if it is not thick, it neither lingers for long, as the dark wine does, nor passes through to the urines in the same way as the wines that are white and at the same time moderately austere. For these are diuretic because they have escaped both forms of passing slowly to the urines, that of the watery wine, which it has undergone on account of coldness, and that of the dark wine, on account of its thickness. For the white and austere wine is least hot, as wines go, and on this account suitable for the fluxes in the belly; yet such a wine is not sufficiently diuretic. Perhaps, then, comparing the dark wine with this one, he says that it is less diuretic.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τὸν μέλανά (last sentence: the lemma compares the sweet wine, so μέλανα may be corrupt for γλυκὺν; translated as transmitted), ὡς ἐν οἴνοις (rendered 'as wines go')

For it has been said earlier that a vinous wine is, and is properly called, the one opposed to the watery wines; it must therefore heat strongly, fill the head, produce derangements, and increase the feverish heat. But such 640 a wine is not white, either when new or when aged, because no other of the wines that are aged remains white either, even if at the outset it was as much of that sort as possible; rather it always becomes more tawny than it was, and finally even yellow. Hippocrates therefore seems to have called the austere wine 'white vinous'. This wine is not diuretic either: if such a wine is thick, it lingers about the hypochondrium, and if it is not thick, it neither lingers for long, as the dark wine does, nor passes through to the urine in the same way as the wines that are at once white and moderately austere. For these are diuretic because they have escaped both kinds of slow passage to the urine: that of the watery wine, which it undergoes through its coldness, and that of the dark wine, through its thickness. For white and austere wine is the least hot, as wines go, and for this reason it is suitable for fluxes of the belly, yet such a wine is not sufficiently diuretic. Perhaps, then, it is by comparing the dark wine with this one that he says it is less diuretic.

641 But the sweet wine is more apt to bring up sputum than the other. Which "other" does he mean? Is it the one set before us, in comparison with which he said of the sweet wine, "for indeed this one is on the whole less diuretic than the vinous white"? Or not this one only, but simply every wine that is not sweet? Since this is unclear so far as this wording goes, and since it is more plausible that his discourse now concerns the vinous white, yet another obscurity arises in addition, because it is not altogether clear what the vinous white is—whether the one that heats vehemently or the one that is excessively astringent, which the Greeks also call stryphnos (harsh), while they call the more moderate one austēros (dry). However matters stand with respect to the wording, I shall state the truth of the text before us. Of all wines the sweet is most apt to bring up sputum, whenever it is not very thick, just as also when it has not departed far toward the tawny, so that its thickness and its sweetness have been dissolved.

642 But the tawny wine is bad with regard to benefit in the fever, since it is considerably hot. And next after the sweet wine that is not extremely thick, the watery wine is suitable for the bringing-up of the matters from the lung, since it strengthens the faculty and moistens and wets the humours moderately; and the same wine is also given to those with fever with less danger than all the other wines. "And in those for whom it is thirst-producing when drunk, it would bring up less in these than the other wine; but in those for whom it is not thirst-producing, it would bring up more than the other." By "the other wine" he now meant the one that is not thirst-producing at all. And why the thirst-producing wine brings up the sputa less, you will easily discover if you bear in mind what has been said before.

ἡ γάρ τοι δίψα παραμένει τισὶ τοῖς τὸν γλυκὺν οἶνον πίνουσι, διὰ τὸ τὴν πυρώδη θερμασίαν ἐν αὐτοῖς εἶναι κακοηθεστέραν· οὖσα δὲ 643 τοιαύτη, ξηραίνουσα τὸ πτύελον ἐργάζεται παχύτερόν τε καὶ γλισχρότερον· ὅτι δὲ τὸ τοιοῦτον δυσανάγωγόν ἐστιν οὐδὲ λόγου δεῖται. Ὁ δὲ λευκὸς οἰνώδης οἶνος ἐπῄνηται μὲν καὶ ἔψεκται τὰ πλεῖστα καὶ τὰ μέγιστα ἤδη ἐν τῇ τοῦ γλυκέος οἴνου διηγήσει· ἐς δὲ κύστιν μᾶλλον πόριμος ἐὼν τοῦ ἑτέρου καὶ διουρητικὸς δὲ ὢν καὶ καταῤῥηκτικὸς ἀεὶ, πολλὰ προσωφελέοι ἂν ἐν ταύτῃσι τῇσι νούσοισι· καὶ γὰρ εἰ πρὸς ἄλλα ἀνεπιτηδειότερος τοῦ ἑτέρου πέφυκεν, ἀλλ᾿ ὅμως ἡ κατὰ τὴν κύστιν κάθαρσις ὑπ᾿ αὐτοῦ γιγνομένη ῥύεται, ἢν προτρέπηται ὁκοῖον δεῖ. καλὰ δὲ ταῦτα τεκμήριά ἐστι τῆς περὶ οἴνου ὠφελείης καὶ βλάβης, ὁκόσα ἀκαταμάθητα ἦν τοῖσιν ἐμεῦ γεραιτέροισιν.

For thirst does persist in some of those who drink the sweet wine, because the fiery heat in them is more malignant; and being 643 such, by drying the sputum it renders it thicker and more viscous; and that sputum of this kind is hard to bring up requires no argument. And these are fine pieces of evidence concerning the benefit and harm from wine, all of which had gone unlearned by those older than myself.

— austere ones — in these diseases you would use for the following purposes: 645 if heaviness of the head were not present, nor any touching of the phrenes (mind), nor the sputum hindered in its ascent, nor the urine held back, but the stools were rather watery and more full of scrapings; in such cases indeed it would be most fitting to change over from the white wine, and in whatever cases are similar to these. The dark wine, however, that is austere without being sweet is useful for the conditions of the belly, whenever either more than is fitting passes down through it, or scraping-like matter does; for then it is suitable, just as the austere white wine is too, and all the 646 other astringent drugs. But the one that has a single quality is twofold in respect of each colour, and the tawny affects the head and the mind more, because it is also hotter than the dark; and by the same reasoning it also increases fevers, for in these, among wines, only the watery ones are suitable.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φρενῶν

Reasonably, then, he forbade the use of the astringent wines, whether they are tawny in colour or dark, in cases of heaviness of the head and injuries of the mind, that is, wherever the fever has laid hold of these parts; and in addition he says that such wines are also unsuitable in those diseases of the thorax and lung in which the sputum is held back, 647 and when the urine is retained, evidently on account of their thickness and their astringency. One must understand beforehand that it will harm all the upper parts and the parts about the bladder less, if it is more watery; but the parts about the intestine it will benefit even more, if it is more unmixed. The more unmixed wine, as he has said, is more suitable for the parts about the intestine; reasonably, then, it benefits them by reason of its astringency, through which it also had its capacity to help at all; but when it is mixed watery, it loses this astringency, and for this reason it benefits them less. And just as it benefits these parts more by reason of its astringency when it is more unmixed, so it harms the parts about the thorax and the head more; and these are what he indicated when he said, "it harms all the upper parts."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐν δὲ ταῖς καρηβαρίαις (the δὲ is awkward after the preceding clause; possibly carried over from the Hippocratic lemma), φρενῶν (rendered 'mind'; could also be 'wits' or 'senses'), κᾀκεῖνα (construction loose: accusative of respect / 'in those diseases'), ἀκρητέστερος / ἀκρατέστερος ('more unmixed', i.e. less diluted, stronger)

With good reason, then, he forbade the use of austere wines, whether they are tawny in colour or dark, in cases of heaviness of the head and of damage to the mind (φρενῶν), that is, wherever the fever has affected these parts. And further he says 647 that such wines are unsuitable also in those diseases of the thorax and lung in which the sputum is held back, and when the urine is retained, evidently on account of their thickness and their astringency. 'One must understand beforehand that it will harm all the upper parts, and those in the region of the bladder, less if it is more watery; but it will benefit the parts in the intestine more if it is more unmixed.' The more unmixed wine, as he has said, is more suitable for the conditions in the intestine. With good reason, then, it benefits them by reason of its astringency, through which it had its capacity to help at all. But when it is mixed so as to be watery, it dissolves this astringency, and for this reason benefits them less. And just as it benefits these parts more by reason of its astringency when it is more unmixed, so it harms the parts in the thorax and the head more. These are what he indicated when he said: 'it harms all the upper parts.'

And it also harms those bodies that need to be purged through the urine; for the austere 648 wine is retentive of all the excretions. Having said these things about wines, Hippocrates passes straight on to melikraton, having set out nearly all the other points concerning wines, but leaving one unworked-out, which I went through as a side matter: that for those with fever the most suitable wine is the watery one. This is the wine white in colour, thin in consistency, having no manifest quality of those that belong to the other wines—neither austerity, nor harshness, nor sweetness, nor pungency, nor odour; for it belongs to such a wine alone of all wines to have escaped both the faults of water and those of wine. Some such wines are produced in each nation: in Italy the slack Sabine, which they also give to those with fever; in Asia the Titacazene and the Tibine.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οἷον εἴρηκα, ἐνίῃ, φρενῶν

And having heard its distinguishing marks, you will easily find wines of this kind among each people, in those regions where very thin and watery wines are produced; I at any rate have found wines of this kind in Cilicia and Phoenicia and Palestine and Scyros and Crete; but nevertheless, as I have said, I have observed them in all regions, and — what is 649 more remarkable — even in Egypt; for it is thick wines, possessing odour and exceedingly strong, that are produced in the hot regions; whereas in Italy and Bithynia and Asia it is nothing remarkable that many watery wines are produced. One might inquire why Hippocrates omitted to speak separately about such wines in the discourse that has been completed, although they are less troublesome to those with fever than all the others. For it is not even possible to say this, that he was ignorant of such a wine; he himself at any rate writes the following a little later, in the discussion of melikraton: 'but also than thin wine, which bears little water and is odourless, in one respect stronger, in another also weaker'; he said these things comparing melikraton with thin and oligophoros wine, that is, wine that bears little water, and odourless. Having, then, clearly and precisely stated the distinguishing marks of the vinous wine, and having gone through how it affects both the head and the mind (phrenes), he considered the teaching to be sufficient also concerning its opposite, the watery wine, since we are able to reason out that the opposite properties belong to it.

Having heard its distinguishing marks, you will easily find such wines in each nation, in those regions where very thin and watery wines are produced. I at any rate found such wines in Cilicia, Phoenicia, Palestine, Scyros and Crete; but nevertheless I have seen wine of the kind I have described in all regions, and, what is 649 more remarkable, even in Egypt; for thick wines, wines that have an odour and are very strong, are produced in hot regions. But in Italy, Bithynia and Asia it is no wonder that many watery wines are produced. One might ask why Hippocrates, in the account he has completed, left out any separate treatment of such wines, even though they are less harmful to the febrile than all the others. For one cannot even say that he did not know such a wine: he himself, at any rate, a little later, in his account of melicrat, writes as follows: 'but also, compared with a wine that is thin, of little bearing (oligophoros) and without odour, it is in some respects stronger and in some respects weaker.' He said this comparing melicrat with a wine that is thin and oligophoros, that is, one that bears little water, and odourless. Having, then, clearly and precisely stated the distinguishing marks of the vinous wine, and having explained that it affects both the head and the mind, he thought this was sufficient teaching also about its opposite, the watery wine, since we are able to reason that the opposite properties belong to it.

650 But melikraton drunk throughout the whole disease, in acute diseases, is on the whole less suitable for the bitter-bilious and for those with large viscera than for those who are not such. For if the disease is not one of those affecting the chest and lung, the patient having an acute fever, and the strength is robust and the disease is not going to extend beyond the fifth day, melikraton drunk alone is sufficient in itself to enable the patient to hold out.

τοῖς μὲν οὖν ἄλλοις ἅπασιν ἐπιτηδειότατόν ἐστιν ἐπὶ τοῖς τοιούτοις νοσήμασι τὸ μελίκρατον, ἦττον δὲ τοῖς πικροχόλοις ὠφέλιμον· ἐκχολοῦται γὰρ ἐν αὐτοῖς, ἐὰν μὴ φθάσῃ διεξελθεῖν ἢ κατ᾿ ἔντερον ἢ κύστιν, ὡς εἴ γε διεξέλθοι, πρὸς τῷ 651 μὴ βλάψαι μηδὲν ὀνίνησι μέγιστα, συνυπάγεται γὰρ αὐτῷ χολώδη περιττώματα. συνεδείξατο γοῦν τῷ λόγῳ τῷδε καὶ περὶ τῆς κράσεως αὐτοῦ· συμφέρει γὰρ αὑτοῦ ὑδαρέστερον ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων φύσεων δίδοσθαι τὸ μελίκρατον, ὅπως μήτε διψῶδες εἴη μήτε χολοποιόν. ὥσπερ δὲ τοῖς πικροχόλοις, οὕτως καὶ τοῖς μεγαλοσπλάγχνοις οὐκ ἐπιτήδειον εἶναί φησιν αὐτό· μεγαλοσπλάγχνους δὲ νῦν εἶπεν οὐχ οἷς φύσει μεγάλα σπλάγχνα εἰσὶν, ἀλλ᾿ οἷς κατά τι πάθος εἰς ὄγκον ᾔρθη σκιῤῥούμενα καὶ φλεγμαίνοντα καὶ οἰδισκόμενα. μοχθηρὸν γὰρ ἐν τούτοις γίγνεται, μὴ δυνάμενόν γε διεξέρχεσθαι ταχέως αὐτὰ, ἐκχολούμενόν τε ῥᾳδίως καὶ μάλιστα ἐν ταῖς φλεγμοναῖς διὰ πλῆθος τῆς θερμασίας, ὡς ἔν γε τοῖς ἄλλοις παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκοις ἧττόν ἐστι βλαβερόν. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αὑτοῦ ὑδαρέστερον

For all the others, then, melikraton is most suitable in such diseases, but it is less beneficial to the bitter-bilious; for in them it is turned into bile, unless it first passes out, either by way of the intestine or by way of the bladder, since if it does pass out, in addition to 651 doing no harm it is of the greatest benefit, for bilious residues are carried off together with it. At any rate, by this statement he also indicated something about its mixture: for in the case of such natures it is advantageous that the melikraton be given more watery than it otherwise is, so that it may be neither thirst-provoking nor bile-producing. And just as for the bitter-bilious, so too for those with large viscera he says that it is not suitable; and by 'those with large viscera' he now meant not those whose viscera are large by nature, but those in whom they have been raised into a swelling through some affection, becoming scirrhous or inflamed or oedematous. For in these it becomes harmful, since it cannot pass through them quickly and is readily turned into bile, and especially so in inflammations because of the abundance of heat, whereas in the other swellings contrary to nature it is less harmful. 'Yet it is less thirst-provoking than sweet wine.'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὑτοῦ (in συμφέρει γὰρ αὑτοῦ ὑδαρέστερον: taken as 'than it usually is', i.e. comparative genitive 'than itself'; possibly corrupt for αὐτοῦ), οἰδισκόμενα (rare form; rendered 'oedematous'), κράσεως (rendered 'mixing', i.e. the proportion of the mixture)

At any rate, by this statement he also indicated at the same time something about its mixing; for in such natures it is beneficial for the honey-water to be given more watery than it usually is, so that it may be neither thirst-provoking nor bile-producing. And just as for the bitter-bilious, so also for those with large viscera he says it is not suitable. By 'those with large viscera' he here meant not those who have large viscera by nature, but those in whom, through some affection, the viscera have been raised into a swelling, becoming scirrhous, inflamed and oedematous. For in these it becomes harmful, since it cannot pass quickly through them and is readily turned into bile, especially in inflammations because of the abundance of heat; whereas in the other swellings contrary to nature it is less harmful. It is, however, less thirst-provoking than sweet wine.

πνεύμονός τε γὰρ μαλθακτικόν ἐστι καὶ πτυέλου ἀναγωγὸν μετρίως καὶ βηχὸς 652 παρηγορικόν· ἔχει γὰρ σμηγματῶδές τι ὃ μᾶλλον τοῦ μετρίου καταγλισχραίνει τὸ πτύελον. Διὰ τί μὲν ἧττον οἴνου ἐστὶ γλυκέος διψῶδες ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηταί μοι, δεικνύντι καθ᾿ ὅσον μέν εἰσιν ἄμφω γλυκέα, κατὰ τοσοῦτον ἐκχολοῦσθαι ῥᾳδίως αὐτά· καθ᾿ ὅσον δὲ λεπτότερόν τ᾿ ἐστὶ καὶ ποριμώτερον, ὡς ἰέναι πάντῃ, τὸ μελίκρατον, κατὰ τοῦτο ἧττον αὐτοῦ γίνεσθαι διψῶδες· ὡς εἴ γέ ποτε κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα μείνῃ χρόνῳ πλείονι καὶ αὐτὸ διψῶδες οὐχ ἧτον ἔσται τοῦ γλυκέος οἴνου. τουτὶ μὲν αὐτὸ σπανίως συμβαίνει· τὸ δὲ θᾶττον οἴνου γλυκέος διεξέρχεσθαι τὴν γαστέρα καὶ τὸ ἔντερον ἅπαν, ἔτι δὲ καὶ τὸ ἧπαρ ἄχρι νεφρῶν καὶ κύστεως οὐ σπανίως, ἀλλὰ διὰ παντὸς ὑπάρχει τῷ μελικράτῳ. διὰ ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἧττόν ἐστι διψῶδες τοῦ γλυκέος οἴνου· ὁ δὲ Ἱπποκράτης οὐχ οὕτως εἶπεν, ἀλλ᾿ ἑτέρως ταὐτὸν ἐνδειξάμενος αὐτοῦ τὴν δύναμιν ἐξ ὧν ἐργάζεται κατὰ τὸν πνεύμονα· δῆλον γὰρ ὡς οὐκ ἂν εἰργάζετο ταῦτα, μὴ διεξερχόμενόν γε καὶ διαῤῥύπτον αὐτοῦ τὰς ὁδούς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καταγλισχραίνει, σμηγματῶδές, ποριμώτερον

For it is softening of the lung, moderately brings up sputum, and is soothing of cough; 652 for it has something detergent in it, which renders the sputum viscous more than is moderate. Why it is less thirst-provoking than sweet wine has been stated by me earlier, when I showed that insofar as both are sweet, to that extent they are easily turned into bile; but insofar as melikraton is thinner and more penetrative, so as to pass everywhere, in this respect it becomes less thirst-provoking than the other; since, if ever it remains in the stomach for a longer time, it too will be no less thirst-provoking than sweet wine. This latter, however, happens rarely; whereas passing more quickly than sweet wine through the stomach and the whole intestine, and further through the liver as far as the kidneys and bladder, belongs to melikraton not rarely but always. For these reasons, then, it is less thirst-provoking than sweet wine; but Hippocrates did not put it in this way, but indicated the same thing differently, pointing to its power from the things it effects in the lung; for it is clear that it would not effect these things if it did not pass through and thoroughly cleanse its passages.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καταγλισχραίνει, σμηγματῶδές, μᾶλλον τοῦ μετρίου, ποριμώτερον, αὐτοῦ τὰς ὁδούς

For it is emollient of the lung, moderately promotes the bringing up of sputum, and 652 is soothing to cough; for it has something detergent, which renders the sputum more viscid than is moderate. Why it is less productive of thirst than sweet wine has been stated by me earlier, when I showed that in so far as both are sweet, to that extent they are readily turned into bile; but in so far as honey-water is thinner and more penetrating, so as to pass everywhere, to that extent it becomes less productive of thirst than the wine. So if it ever remains in the stomach for a longer time, it too will be no less productive of thirst than sweet wine. This in itself happens rarely. But passing more quickly than sweet wine through the stomach and the whole intestine, and further also through the liver as far as the kidneys and bladder, belongs to honey-water not rarely but always. For these reasons, then, it is less productive of thirst than sweet wine. Hippocrates, however, did not put it thus. He indicated the same thing in another way, showing its power from what it effects in the lung. For it is clear that it would not effect these things unless it passed through and cleansed the lung's passages.

Greek (Kühn)

πτυέλου δ᾿ ἀναγωγὸν 653 οὐχ ἁπλῶς εἶπε τὸ μελίκρατον, ἀλλ᾿ ὅτι μετρίως προσέγραψε. τὸ γὰρ ἰσχυρῶς ἀνάγειν οὐκ ἔχει, καθάπερ τὸ ὀξύμελι· τό γε μὴν παρηγορικὸν ἔχει, μηδ᾿ ὅλως ἔχοντος αὐτὸ τοῦ ὀξυμέλιτος· ἐπεγείρει γὰρ ἐκεῖνο τὰς βῆχας, ἐρεθίζον τε καὶ τραχῦνον, ἐκ τοῦ ῥύπτειν σφοδρῶς. μελικράτῳ δὲ καὶ μάλιστα τῷ ὑδαρεῖ τῆς ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως ὀλιγίστης μέτεστιν· καλεῖται δ᾿ ἡ τοιαύτη καὶ σμηκτικὴ, καὶ νῦν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἀπ᾿ ἐκείνης ἐποιήσατο τὸ σμηγματῶδες ὄνομα, περὶ τοῦ μελικράτου γράψας, ἔχει γὰρ σμηγματῶδές τι· μετρίας αὐτῷ μετεῖναι τῆς τοιαύτης δυνάμεως αἰνιξάμενος ἐκ τοῦ προσθεῖναι τῷ σμηγματῶδες τὸ τὶ, βραχύτητα δηλοῦν οἷς ἂν οὕτω προσκέηται. τοῦτ᾿ οὖν τὸ βραχὺ τοῦ μελικράτου σμηγματῶδες, οὐχ ὅτι σμηγματῶδες, διὰ τοῦτ᾿ ἄκρατον, ἀλλ᾿ ὅτι βραχύ· δεῖ γὰρ ἐπὶ τοῦ γλίσχρου πτυέλου σφοδρῶς εἶναι σμηγματῶδες τὸ φάρμακον, ἀποῤῥύψαι δυνάμενον τὸν ἐνιζηκότα τοῖς βρόγχοις τοῦ πνεύμονος γλίσχρον χυμόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄκρατον

And he did not say simply that melikraton brings up sputum, 653 but added that it does so moderately. For it does not have the capacity to bring it up strongly, as oxymel does; it does, however, possess the soothing quality, which oxymel does not possess at all, for that stirs up coughs, irritating and roughening, as a result of its cleansing vigorously. Melikraton, and especially the watery kind, has only the very smallest share of the cleansing faculty; such a faculty is also called smectic (scouring), and here Hippocrates formed from it the term "smegmatic" (soap-like), writing about melikraton, "for it has something smegmatic," hinting that it has a share in a moderate degree of such a faculty by adding to "smegmatic" the word "something," which indicates smallness in whatever it is thus attached to. This slight smegmatic quality of melikraton, then, is of no avail (ἄκρατον) not because it is smegmatic, but because it is slight; for in the case of viscous sputum the drug must be strongly smegmatic, capable of scouring away the viscous humour that has settled in the bronchi of the lung.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄκρατον (literally 'unmixed'; makes poor sense here, possibly corrupt, e.g. for ἄπρακτον 'ineffective'; rendered by sense), σμηγματῶδες (rendered 'soap-like'), σμηκτική (rendered 'abstersive')

He did not say simply that melicrat brings up 653 sputum, but added "moderately". For it does not have the property of bringing up strongly, as oxymel does; it does, however, have the soothing property, which oxymel does not have at all, for that provokes coughs, irritating and roughening by its vehement cleansing. Melicrat, and above all the watery kind, has only a very small share of the cleansing faculty. Such a faculty is also called abstersive, and here Hippocrates formed from it the word "soap-like" (σμηγματῶδες), writing of melicrat "for it has something soap-like". He hinted that it has a moderate share of such a faculty by adding "something" to "soap-like", since this word indicates smallness in whatever it is so attached to. This slight soap-like quality of melicrat, then, is on that account ineffective (ἄκρατον), not because it is soap-like but because it is slight. For in the case of viscous sputum the remedy must be vehemently soap-like, able to wash away the viscous humour that has settled in the bronchi of the lung.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ δὲ μεταξὺ θώρακός τε καὶ πνεύμονος εἴη τοιοῦτός τις ἠθροσμένος, ἔτι δὴ καὶ μᾶλλόν ἐστι χρεία 654 πρὸς τὸ καλῶς ἀναπτυσθῆναι τμητικῆς τε καὶ σμηκτικῆς δυνάμεως, ἥτις καὶ ἀποῤῥύψει τε τοῦ ὑπεζωκότος αὐτὸ καὶ διατέμνει καὶ λεπτύνει· μεταληφθῆναι γὰρ ἄλλως ἀδύνατόν ἐστι τὸ τοιοῦτον, διὰ τοῦ περιέχοντος ὑμένος τὸ σπλάγχνον ἐς τὰ βρογχία τοῦ πνεύμονος. οὐ καλῶς οὖν ἐχρήσατο τῇ λέξει, προσθεὶς τῷ, ἔχει γὰρ σμηγματῶδές τι, τὸ ἐφεξῆς εἰρημένον, ὃ μᾶλλον τοῦ μετρίου καταγλισχραίνει τὸ πτύελον· ἄμεινον γὰρ ἦν εἰπεῖν, ἔχει γὰρ σμηγματῶδές τι βραχὺ τὸ μελίκρατον, ὃ μᾶλλον τοῦ μετρίου πολλάκις ἐπιτρέπει τῷ πτυέλῳ καταγλισχραίνεσθαι. οὐ γὰρ διὰ τὸ μελίκρατον συμβαίνει τῷ πτυέλῳ καταγλισχραίνεσθαι, ἀλλὰ τοῦτο μὲν οἰκεῖον ὑπάρχει τῷ γε τοιούτῳ πτυέλῳ, τὸ μελίκρατον δὲ οὐδὲν ὀνίνησιν, ἀλλὰ διαμένειν ἐᾷ τὴν τοιαύτην φύσιν τοῦ περιττώματος. διὰ τοῦτ᾿ οὖν ἄμεινον ἦν εἰπεῖν μηδὲν ὀνίνασθαι τὸ γλίσχρον πτύελον, οὐ γίγνεσθαι τοιοῦτο ὑπὸ τοῦ μελικράτου· τὸ γὰρ καταγλισχραίνειν τὸ πρὸς Ἱπποκράτους εἰρημένον ἔμφασιν ἔχει τοῦ γίνεσθαι τοιοῦτον ὑπὸ τοῦ μελικράτου τὸ πτύελον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπεζωκότος

But if some such [humour] has collected between the thorax and the lung, there is still greater need, 654 in order for it to be properly coughed up, of a cutting and detergent faculty, which will both scour it away from the lining membrane (hypezokos) and cut it through and thin it; for otherwise it is impossible for such [a humour] to be transferred, through the membrane that surrounds the viscus, into the bronchia of the lung. He therefore did not use the expression well when he added to the words 'for it has something detergent' what is said next, 'which makes the sputum more viscous than is moderate'; for it would have been better to say, 'for melikraton has something slightly detergent, which often allows the sputum to become more viscous than is moderate'. For it is not because of the melikraton that the sputum comes to be viscous; rather, this is proper to sputum of that kind itself, and the melikraton gives no benefit, but allows such a nature of the residue to persist. For this reason, then, it would have been better to say that the viscous sputum receives no benefit, not that it becomes such from the melikraton; for the word 'makes viscous' (καταγλισχραίνειν) used by Hippocrates carries the implication that the sputum becomes such from the melikraton.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπεζωκότος, μεταληφθῆναι, σμηγματῶδές, καταγλισχραίνει

But if some such [humour] should have collected between the thorax and the lung, there is still greater need 654, for it to be properly coughed up, of a cutting and detergent faculty, which will both scour it off from the membrane lining the ribs and cut it through and thin it; for otherwise it is impossible for such matter to be taken across, through the membrane that encloses the viscus, into the bronchia of the lung. He did not, then, use the expression well when he added to 'for it has something detergent' what is said next, 'which makes the sputum viscid more than is moderate'; for it would have been better to say: 'for melicrat has some slight detergent quality, which often allows the sputum to become viscid more than is moderate.' For it is not because of the melicrat that the sputum comes to be made viscid; rather, this is proper to sputum of that kind, while the melicrat gives no benefit but lets such a nature of the residue persist. For this reason, then, it would have been better to say that viscid sputum derives no benefit, not that it becomes such through the melicrat; for 'makes viscid', as stated by Hippocrates, carries the implication that the sputum becomes such through the melicrat.

Greek (Kühn)

655 Ἔστι δὲ καὶ οὐρητικὸν τὸ μελίκρητον ἱκανῶς, ἢν μή τι τῶν ἀπὸ σπλάγχνων κωλύῃ. Λέλεκταί μοι καὶ πρόσθεν ὅτι τῷ διεξέρχεσθαι ῥᾳδίως ἁπάσας τὰς μεταξὺ κοιλίας τε καὶ νεφρῶν ὁδοὺς, οὐρητικόν ἐστι τὸ μελίκρατον· ἐδείχθη δὲ καὶ ὅτι κακοπραγοῦντος τοῦ ἥπατος οὐ προσήκει πίνειν αὐτὸ, βλάπτει δὲ καὶ σπλῆνα φλεγμαίνοντα, καὶ πολὺ δὴ μᾶλλον ἔντερά τε καὶ γαστέρα, ταχέως γὰρ ἐκπυρούμενον ὑπὸ τῆς κατὰ τὴν φλεγμονὴν θερμασίας εἰς χολὴν μεταβάλλει. τάχα γὰρ διὰ τὴν γλυκύτητα χαίρει τὰ σπλάγχνα τῷ μέλιτι, καθάπερ καὶ ἡ γαστὴρ, ὥστε θᾶττόν τε καὶ ἀθροώτερον ἐπισπᾶσθαι· καὶ γὰρ οὖν καὶ τἄλλα γλυκέα ταὐτὸν ἔχειν τούτῳ λογίσαιτ᾿ ἄν τις ὥσπερ πρὸς τὴν κοιλίαν, οὕτω καὶ πρὸς τὰ σπλάγχνα πεπονθέναι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πεπονθέναι

655 The melikraton is also sufficiently diuretic, unless something from the viscera prevents it. It has been said by me also earlier that it is by passing readily through all the passages between the belly and the kidneys that melikraton is diuretic; and it was also shown that when the liver is functioning badly it is not fitting to drink it, and that it harms also an inflamed spleen, and much more indeed the intestines and the stomach, for being quickly fired up by the heat that accompanies the inflammation it changes into bile. For perhaps on account of its sweetness the viscera take pleasure in honey, just as the stomach does too, so that they draw it in both more quickly and more all at once; and indeed one might also reckon that the other sweet things have the same property as this, namely that they are disposed toward the viscera in the same way as toward the belly.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀθροώτερον, ταὐτὸν ἔχειν τούτῳ, πεπονθέναι

655 Honey-water is also sufficiently diuretic, unless something arising from the viscera hinders it. I have said before as well that honey-water is diuretic because it passes easily through all the passages between the belly and the kidneys; and it was also shown that when the liver is faring badly one ought not to drink it, and that it also harms a spleen that is inflamed, and much more so the intestines and the stomach. For, being quickly set ablaze by the heat attending the inflammation, it changes into bile. For perhaps the viscera delight in honey because of its sweetness, just as the stomach also does, so that they draw it in more quickly and more all at once. And indeed one might reckon that the other sweet things too have the same property as this one, being related to the viscera just as they are to the belly.

φανεῖται δ᾿ ἡ γαστὴρ ἥδεσθαι τούτοις· καὶ γὰρ μετὰ τὸ καταπιεῖν αὐτὰ, φανερῶς πώς ἐστιν ἡδομένη πρὸς τῶν εἰλημμένων, ὥσπερ αὖ πάλιν ἀνιωμένη 656 τοῖς τραχύνουσί τε καὶ πικροῖς, οὐκ ἐν αὐτῷ τῷ λαμβάνεσθαι μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ μετὰ τὸ ληφθῆναι ἄχρι χρόνου τινός. ὅθεν οὐδὲ προσφέρεταί τις αὐτὰ, πλὴν εἴ ποτε ὡς φάρμακον, διὰ χυμοὺς παρὰ φύσιν ἠθροισμένους ἐν τῇ γαστρί· κατὰ φύσιν δὲ ἐχούσης, ἀνιαρὰ πάντα αὐτῇ τὰ τοιαῦτά ἐστιν, ἡδέα δὲ μόνα τὰ γλυκέα. καὶ μὲν δὴ κατὰ τὸν καλούμενον λαιμὸν ἐκ τῶν καταπινομένων γλυκέων ἡδονὴ μεγίστη γίνεται, τοῦ κατ᾿ αὐτὸ τὸ χωρίον σώματος οἰκείως ἔχοντος τῷ τοιούτῳ χυμῷ· τὸ δ᾿ αὐτὸ τοῦτ᾿ ἔστι σῶμα καὶ τῆς γαστρὸς ὁ ἔνδον χιτὼν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ ὁ τὴν γλῶτταν ἀμφιεννύς. ἀλλ᾿ ἐν ταύτῃ μὲν ἐνεργεστάτην ἔχει τὴν τῆς γλυκύτητος αἴσθησιν, ἐν δὲ τῷ λαιμῷ τὴν τῆς ἡδονῆς, αἴσθησις δὲ σαφὴς ἐν τῷ παραχρῆμα γίνεται καὶ ἐν τῷ στόματι τῆς γαστρὸς, ἡδομένῳ τοῖς καταπινομένοις γλυκέσιν, ὅταν κατὰ φύσιν ἔχῃ τὸ ζῶον.

And the stomach will appear to take pleasure in these things; for indeed after swallowing them it is in some way manifestly pleased by what has been taken, just as, conversely, it is distressed 656 by things that are roughening and bitter, not only in the very act of taking them but also for some time after they have been taken. Hence no one administers these, except sometimes as a drug, on account of humours gathered contrary to nature in the stomach; but when it is in its natural state, all such things are distressing to it, and sweet things alone are pleasant. And indeed in the so-called gullet (laimos) the greatest pleasure arises from the sweet things being swallowed, since the body in that very region stands in a kindred relation to such a juice; and this same body is also the inner coat of the stomach, just as is the coat that clothes the tongue. But in the latter it has the most vivid perception of sweetness, whereas in the gullet it has that of pleasure; and a clear perception arises immediately also in the mouth of the stomach, which is pleased by the sweet things being swallowed, whenever the animal is in its natural state.

And the stomach will be seen to take pleasure in these; for after it has swallowed them it is, in a way, manifestly pleased by what has been taken, just as, conversely, it is distressed 656 by things that roughen and by bitter things, not only in the very act of their being taken but also after they have been taken, for some time. Moreover, in the so-called gullet the greatest pleasure arises from sweet things that are swallowed, since the body of that very region has an affinity for such a savour; and this same body is also the inner tunic of the stomach, just as it is also the one that clothes the tongue. But in the tongue it has the most active perception of sweetness, while in the gullet it has that of pleasure; and a clear perception arises immediately in the mouth of the stomach as well, which takes pleasure in sweet things that are swallowed, when the animal is in its natural state.

οὕτως οὖν εἰκός ἐστι καὶ τὰ σπλάγχνα χαίρειν τῷ μὲν γλυκεῖ χυμῷ, ταχέως δ᾿ αὐτοῦ καὶ πλεῖστον ἐπισπώμενα, καθ᾿ ὃν μὲν ὑγιαίνει χρόνον, ἀπολαύειν τε καὶ τρέφεσθαι, καθ᾿ ὃν δὲ πυρετώδη 657 τέ ἐστι καὶ φλεγμαίνει, μεταβάλλειν ἐστὶ τὸ χολῶδες αὐτό. ὁρᾶται γοῦν καὶ τὰ καλούμενα συκωτὰ διὰ τῆς τῶν ἰσχάδων ἐδωδῆς, μέγιστά τε καὶ ἥδιστα γιγνόμενα, προφανῶς τοῦ ἥπατος εὐτροφοῦντος ἐπὶ τῇ τῶν γλυκέων ἐδωδῇ· ἀλλ᾿ ἐν ταῖς φλεγμοναῖς τε καὶ τοῖς πυρετοῖς οὐκ εἰς εὐτροφίαν, ἀλλ᾿ εἰς χολῆς γένεσιν, ἡ τῶν τοιούτων προσφορὰ μεταβάλλεται, τοῦ μὲν πυρετώδους θερμοῦ πλεονάζοντος, ἐλλείποντος δὲ τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν, ὅπερ εὔκρατον ἦν. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μεταβάλλειν ἐστὶ τὸ χολῶδες αὐτό, συκωτὰ

So then it is likely that the viscera too rejoice in the sweet humour, and, drawing it in quickly and in very great quantity, during the time when they are healthy enjoy it and are nourished, but during the time when they are feverish 657 and inflamed change it into the bilious. At any rate the so-called sykota (fig-fed livers) are seen, through the eating of dried figs, to become very large and most delicious, the liver evidently being well nourished upon the eating of sweet things; but in inflammations and fevers the administration of such things is converted not into good nourishment but into the generation of bile, since the feverish heat is in excess and the natural heat, which was well-tempered, is deficient. And it is more purgative downward of bilious matters, sometimes good ones, sometimes ones more saturated than is fitting and more frothy; and this sort of thing occurs more in bilious persons and those with large viscera.

In this way, then, it is likely that the viscera too delight in the sweet humour and, drawing it to themselves quickly and in very great quantity, during the time they are healthy they enjoy it and are nourished by it, but during the time they are feverish 657 and inflamed they change it into what is bilious. At any rate, the so-called sykota ('fig-fed livers') are seen to become very large and very pleasant through the eating of dried figs, the liver evidently being well nourished as a result of the eating of sweet things. But in inflammations and fevers the administration of such things changes not into good nourishment but into the generation of bile, since the feverish heat is in excess, while the natural heat, which was well-tempered, is deficient. And it is more apt to evacuate bilious matters downwards, sometimes good ones, sometimes ones more intensely coloured than is opportune and more frothy; but this happens rather in those who are bilious and in those with large viscera.

Greek (Kühn)

Ἀναμνησθῶμεν τῆς προειρημένης λέξεως, ἐν ᾗ παραβάλλων οἴνῳ γλυκεῖ τὸ μελίκρατον ἔλεγε, διψῶδές γε μὴν ἧσσον τοῦ γλυκέος οἴνου· καὶ νῦν οὖν πρὸς ἐκεῖνον εἶπε 658 διαχωρητικώτερον εἶναι κάτω. χολωδέων γὰρ δὴ καὶ ὁ γλυκὺς οἶνος διαχωρητικὸς, ἀλλὰ τό γε μελίκρατον ἐνίοτε μὲν καλῶν τῶν χολωδέων ὑπακτικόν ἐστιν, ἐνίοτε δὲ μοχθηρῶν, ἅπερ ἐστὶν ἱκανῶς ἄκρατα χολώδη, τὰ πυῤῥὰν ἢ ξανθὴν ἔχοντα καὶ παχεῖαν χολήν. αὕτη τοίνυν ἀφρὸν προσλαμβάνει, πολλάκις μὲν κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον, ὃν καὶ τοῖς ἐν πυρὶ θερμαινομένοις σφοδρῶς ἀφρὸς ἐπανθεῖ, πολλάκις δὲ τὸ τοῖς κοπτομένοις ὑγροῖς ὑπὸ πνεύματος. τούτων δὲ τὸ μὲν πρότερον ὧν εἶπον ὑπερβαλλούσης ἐστὶ θερμασίας γνώρισμα, τὸ δὲ δεύτερον, στενοχωρίας τῶν ὀργάνων καὶ ταραχῆς φυσώδους πνεύματος ἐναπειλημμένου, καὶ διέξοδον οὐ ῥᾳδίαν ἔχοντος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸ τοῖς κοπτομένοις ὑγροῖς ὑπὸ πνεύματος

Let us recall the previously cited passage, in which, comparing melikraton with sweet wine, he said, "yet less thirst-provoking than sweet wine"; and so now too it is with reference to that [wine] that he said 658 that it is more purgative downward. For sweet wine too is purgative of bilious matters, but melikraton, for its part, is sometimes productive of evacuation of bilious matters that are good, and sometimes of bad ones, namely those which are excessively unmixed in their biliousness, containing reddish or yellow and thick bile. This [bile], then, takes on foam, often in the same manner in which foam also forms on the surface of things that are being strongly heated over a fire, and often in the manner [that happens] to liquids beaten up by pneuma. Of these things which I have mentioned, the former is a sign of excessive heat, the latter of narrowness of the organs and of a disturbance of flatulent pneuma that has been trapped within and does not have an easy way out.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τὸ τοῖς κοπτομένοις ὑγροῖς (τὸ is probably a corruption of τὸν [sc. λόγον]; rendered so), πυῤῥὰν

Let us recall the previously quoted passage, in which, comparing honey-water with sweet wine, he said: 'less thirst-provoking, however, than sweet wine'. So now too, in relation to that wine, he has said 658 that it is more apt to pass downward. For sweet wine too carries off bilious matters, but honey-water is sometimes evacuant of good bilious matters, and sometimes of bad ones. These are the bilious matters that are quite unmixed, those containing tawny or yellow and thick bile. This bile, then, takes on froth. Often this happens by the same principle by which froth blooms upon things heated vigorously in fire. Often it happens by the principle that holds for liquids beaten by pneuma. Of the two I have mentioned, the former is a sign of excessive heat. The latter is a sign of narrowness of the organs and of a flatulent disturbance of pneuma that is trapped within and has no easy passage out.
