Greek (Kühn)

τοιαῦτα δ᾿ ὑπέρχεται χολώδη τοὐπίπαν ἐν τοῖς συντηκτικοῖς πυρετοῖς, ἐφ᾿ ὧν ἐναντιωτάτη τε καὶ βλαβερωτάτη ἁπάντων σχεδόν ἐστιν ἡ τοῦ μελικράτου πόσις, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τοὐναντίον ἡ τοῦ ψυχροῦ πόσις ὕδατος ὠφελιμωτάτη, μᾶλλον δὲ τὸ τοιοῦτον διαχώρημά φησιν ὑπὸ τοῦ μελικράτου γίνεσθαι, τοῖς φύσει μὲν χολώδεσι, τουτέστι τοῖς θερμοῖς ἀμέτρως, ἐν δὲ ταῖς νόσοις φλεγμονὴν ἔχουσι 659 κατά τι τῶν σπλάγχνων. τὸ γάρ τοι κοινὸν ὄνομα νῦν ἴδιον ἐγένετο τῶν φλεγμαινόντων· οὐ γὰρ δὴ διὰ σκίῤῥον γε ἢ οἴδημα πυρετὸς ἐξάπτεται. πρόδηλον οὖν ὅτι κατὰ τὰς ὀξείας νόσους, ὑπὲρ ὧν ὁ λόγος αὐτῷ ἐστιν, ὅταν ὄγκος ἐν σπλάγχνοις γένηται, φλεγμονῆς ἐστιν, οὐκ οἰδήματος ἢ σκίῤῥου τὸ σύμπτωμα· καὶ τούτους νῦν ὠνόμασε μεγαλοσπλάγχνους. Πτυέλου μὲν οὖν ἀναγωγὴν καὶ πνεύμονος μάλθαξιν τὸ ὑδαρέστερον μελίκρητον ποιέει μᾶλλον· τὰ μέντοι ἀφρώδεα διαχωρήματα καὶ μᾶλλον τοῦ καιροῦ κατακορέως χολώδεα καὶ μᾶλλον θερμὰ τὸ ἄκρητον μᾶλλον τοῦ ὑδαρέος ἄγει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατακορέως

Such bilious matter passes downward, for the most part, in colliquative fevers, in which the drinking of melikraton is the most contrary and most harmful of almost all things, just as, conversely, the drinking of cold water is most beneficial. Rather, however, he says that such a stool is produced by the melikraton in those who are bilious by nature, that is, those who are immoderately hot, and who in their diseases have an inflammation 659 in one of the viscera. For the common name has now become proper to those who are inflamed; for certainly a fever is not kindled on account of a scirrhus or an oedema. It is plain, then, that in the acute diseases, with which his discourse is concerned, when a swelling arises in the viscera, the symptom is one of inflammation, not of oedema or scirrhus; and these persons he has now called 'large-viscera'd' (megalosplanchnoi). The more watery melikreton, then, produces rather the bringing up of sputum and softening of the lung; but the frothy stools, which are more intensely (κατακορέως) bilious than is fitting and hotter, the undiluted brings down more than the watery.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τὸ γάρ τοι κοινὸν ὄνομα νῦν ἴδιον ἐγένετο τῶν φλεγμαινόντων, μᾶλλον τοῦ καιροῦ κατακορέως

Bilious matters of this kind are, for the most part, passed in colliquative fevers, in which the drinking of honey-water is very nearly the most contrary and most harmful of all things, just as, conversely, the drinking of cold water is the most beneficial. He says, rather, that such an evacuation is produced by honey-water in those who are bilious by nature, that is, immoderately hot, and who in their diseases have an inflammation 659 in one of the viscera. For the common name has here become specific to those whose viscera are inflamed, since fever is certainly not kindled on account of a scirrhus or an oedema. It is quite clear, then, that in the acute diseases, which are the subject of his discussion, whenever a swelling arises in the viscera, the symptom belongs to inflammation, not to oedema or scirrhus; and it is these people that he has here called 'those with enlarged viscera'. The more watery honey-water is the better at producing the bringing up of sputum and the softening of the lung; but the frothy evacuations, and those that are excessively bilious beyond what is fitting, and those that are hotter, the undiluted kind draws off more than the watery.

Greek (Kühn)

Λέλεκται καὶ πρόσθεν ὡς ἐκχολουμένου τοῦ μέλιτος ἐν ταῖς ὀξείαις νόσοις καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο βλάπτοντος ἡμᾶς, βραδυπόρου δ᾿ ὄντος τοῦ ὕδατος καὶ μένοντος ἐπὶ πλεῖστον 660 ἐν τοῖς ὑποχονδρίοις καὶ διὰ τοῦτ᾿ ὄντος φευκτοῦ· τὸ μικτὸν ἔξ ἀμφοῖν ἐπιτήδειον γίνεται, κατὰ τὴν τοιαύτην κρᾶσιν συντιθέμενον, ὅταν ὕδατι πλείονι μέλιτος μιχθῇ τοσοῦτον, ὡς ὁδοποιεῖν τε καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἀνάδοσιν ἄγειν αὐτό. τά τε γὰρ οὖρα ποριμώτερα γίνεται καὶ ἡ τῶν πτυέλων ἀναγωγὴ θᾶττον, οὕτω κερασθέντων αὐτῶν· εἰ δὲ πολὺ τοῦ μέλιτος μιχθείη, δίψαν τε ποιεῖ καὶ γλισχραίνει τὸ πτύελον. καὶ διὰ ταῦτα μὲν ἐπιτηδειότερόν ἐστι τὸ ὑδαρέστερον μελίκρατον, εἴς τε τὴν τῶν πτυέλων ἀναγωγὴν καὶ τὴν τῶν οὔρων διέξοδον, τὸ δ᾿ ἀκρατέστερον εἰς τὰς τῆς γαστρὸς ὑποχωρήσεις· ὅσῳ γὰρ ἂν ἐρεθιστικώτερον ᾖ, τοσούτῳ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ θᾶττον αὐτὴν ὑπάγει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It has also been said earlier that, since honey turns into bile in acute diseases and in this respect harms us, while water is slow to pass and remains for a very long time 660 in the hypochondria and on this account is to be avoided, the mixture of both becomes suitable, when it is compounded in the following blending: whenever to a greater quantity of water only so much honey is mixed as to open a way for it and lead it toward distribution. For both the urines become more readily passed and the bringing up of the sputa quicker when they are mixed in this way; but if much honey is mixed in, it produces thirst and makes the sputum viscous. And for these reasons the more watery melikraton is more suitable for the bringing up of the sputa and the passage of the urines, while the less diluted one is more suitable for the evacuations of the belly; for the more irritating it is, the more and the faster it moves the belly downward.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁδοποιεῖν, ποριμώτερα

It has also been said before that, since honey turns to bile in acute diseases and in this respect harms us, while water is slow of passage and remains for a very long time 660 in the hypochondria and for this reason is to be avoided, the mixture made from both becomes suitable, when it is compounded according to such a blend, namely when so much honey is mixed with a larger quantity of water as both to open a way for it and to carry it on to distribution. For the urine becomes more freely passed, and the bringing up of the sputa quicker, when they are mixed in this way; but if much of the honey is mixed in, it both produces thirst and makes the sputum viscid. And for these reasons the more watery melicrat is more suitable both for the bringing up of the sputa and for the passage of the urine, while the less diluted is more suitable for the evacuations of the belly; for the more irritating it is, the more and the more quickly it moves the belly downwards.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ διὰ τῶν κλυστήρων δὲ ἐνιέμενον, ὅσῳπερ ἂν ἀκρατέστερον ᾖ, τοσοῦτον μᾶλλον ἐκκριτικὸν γίνεται τὸ τοιοῦτον· καὶ τῶν κατακορέως χολωδῶν, τουτέστι τῶν ἱκανῶς τοιούτων καὶ τῶν ἀφρωδῶν διαχωρημάτων αἴτιον ὑπάρχει· τὸ γὰρ ἐπισπᾶσθαι τὰ τοιαῦτα μᾶλλον ἔχει τῷ δάκνειν τε καὶ ῥύπτειν τὸ ἔντερον· ἔτι δὲ καὶ αὐτὸ μεταβάλλον εἰς χολὴν ξανθὴν συναύξει καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο τῶν χολωδῶν διαχωρημάτων τὸ πλῆθος, ὅθεν οὐδ᾿ ἐπαινεῖ 661 τὴν χρῆσιν αὐτοῦ. δηλοῖ δ᾿ ἐκ τοῦ προσθεῖναι καὶ μᾶλλον τοῦ καιροῦ κατακορέως χολώδεα· τὸ γὰρ μᾶλλον τοῦ καιροῦ σύνηθες αὐτῷ λέγειν ἀντὶ τοῦ μᾶλλον τοῦ προσήκοντος. τὸ δὲ ἀφρῶδες διαχώρημα καὶ κατὰ τὸ προγνωστικὸν ἐμέμψατο· καὶ τὰ κατακορέως δὲ, τουτέστιν ἀκράτως χολώδη, μοχθηρά· χρὴ γὰρ οὐ κατακορέως, οὐδ᾿ ἀκράτως, ἀλλὰ μετρίως αὐτὰ τοιαῦτα εἶναι. μεμνημένος οὖν καὶ σὺ τούτων, ὅταν ἤτοι μελικράτου ποθέντος ἢ καὶ χωρὶς αὐτοῦ διαχωρεῖ τις ἀφρώδη τε ἅμα καὶ κατακορέως χολώδη, συντηκτικὸν εἶναι τὸν πυρετὸν ἴσθι καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀφίστασο τῆς τοῦ μελικράτου χρήσεως, ἐπὶ δὲ τὸν τῆς πτισάνης ἧκε χυλὸν, ἐπιτηρῶν ἰδεῖν τι γνώρισμα κᾂν βραχὺ, διὰ τῶν οὔρων, τῆς τοῦ νοσήματος πέψεως· ἐν ἐκείνῳ γὰρ τῷ καιρῷ διδοὺς καὶ τὸ ψυχρὸν ὕδωρ ὀνήσεις μέγιστα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And when injected through clysters, too, the more undiluted it is, the more evacuant such a mixture becomes; and it is the cause of stools that are intensely bilious, that is, of those that are considerably such, and of frothy stools. For it is more capable of drawing such matters by its biting and scouring of the intestine; and moreover, itself changing into yellow bile, it also in this way increases the quantity of the bilious stools, whence he does not even approve 661 its use. He makes this clear by adding "and intensely bilious more than is seasonable"; for it is customary for him to say "more than is seasonable" instead of "more than is fitting". The frothy stool he censured also in the Prognostic; and those that are intensely, that is unmixedly, bilious are bad; for they ought to be such not intensely, nor unmixedly, but moderately. So you too, mindful of these things, whenever, either after melikraton has been drunk or even without it, someone passes stools that are at once frothy and intensely bilious, know that the fever is colliquative, and on this account desist from the use of melikraton and proceed to the juice of ptisane, watching to see some sign, even a slight one, through the urines, of the concoction of the disease; for if at that moment you give cold water as well, you will benefit the patient most greatly.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατακορέως, μᾶλλον τοῦ καιροῦ, συντηκτικὸν

And when it is injected by means of clysters, the less diluted such a thing is, the more excretory it becomes; and it is the cause of intensely bilious evacuations, that is, those that are sufficiently such, and of frothy ones. For it has the property of drawing out such matters the more by biting and scouring the intestine; and further, itself changing into yellow bile, it thereby also increases the quantity of the bilious evacuations, which is why he does not commend 661 its use. He makes this clear by adding 'and more than is opportune intensely bilious'; for 'more than is opportune' is his habitual way of saying 'more than is fitting'. The frothy evacuation he also censured in the Prognostic; and the intensely bilious ones, that is, unmixedly bilious, are bad; for they ought to be such not intensely, nor unmixedly, but moderately. You too, then, remembering these things, whenever someone, either after melicrat has been drunk or even without it, passes evacuations that are at once frothy and intensely bilious, know that the fever is colliquative, and for this reason refrain from the use of melicrat, and turn to the juice of ptisan, watching to see some sign, even a small one, through the urine, of the concoction of the disease; for at that time, by giving cold water as well, you will be of the greatest benefit.

Greek (Kühn)

Τὸ δὲ τοιοῦτον διαχώρημα ἔχει μὲν καὶ ἄλλα σίνεα μεγάλα (οὔτε γὰρ ἐξ ὑποχονδρίων καῦμα σβέννυσιν, ἀλλ᾿ ὁρμᾷ· 662 δυσφορίην τε καὶ ῥιπτασμὸν τῶν μελέων ποιέει) ἑλκῶδες δ᾿ ἐστι καὶ ἐντέρου καὶ ἕδρης· ἀλεξητήρια δὲ τουτέων γεγράψεται. Σύνηθες αὐτῷ τὰς βλάβας σίνεα λέγειν, ὡς κᾀν τῷ περὶ ἀγμῶν καὶ ἄρθρων ἐδείχθη· καὶ γάρτοι σίνειν λέγουσιν οἱ Ἴωνες ἀντὶ τοῦ βλάπτειν· ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ βλαβερὰ σινόμορα πολλάκις αὐτὸς ὠνόμασε. μεγάλας οὖν βλάβας ἔχειν φησὶ τὸ προειρημένον διαχώρημα, τουτέστι τὸ κατακορὲς χολῶδες ἅμα τῷ καὶ ἀφρῶδες εἶναι. σύνηθες δ᾿ αὐτῷ πολλάκις ἐπὶ τῶν σημείων χρῆσθαι ταῖς τῶν αἰτίων τε καὶ βλαπτομένων σωμάτων φωναῖς· τὸ γοῦν τοιόνδε διαχώρημα κάλλιον ἦν εἰπεῖν σημαίνειν, ἀλλ᾿ ἔχειν εἶπε· καίτοι τὰ μὲν πεπονθότα σώματα τὰς βλάβας ἴσχει, τὰ δ᾿ αἴτια ποιεῖ, τὰ δὲ σημεῖα δηλοῖ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Such a stool has also other great harms (for it does not quench the burning heat from the hypochondria, but rouses it; 662 and it produces distress and tossing of the limbs), and it is ulcerating both of the intestine and of the seat; but remedies against these will be written. It is his custom to call injuries sinea ("harms"), as was shown also in the treatise On Fractures and On Joints; for indeed the Ionians say sinein in place of blaptein ("to injure"); and he himself has also often named harmful things sinomora. He says, then, that the aforementioned stool has great harms, that is, the stool that is intensely bilious together with its being frothy. It is his custom often, in the case of signs, to use the expressions proper to causes and to the bodies that are being harmed; at any rate it would have been better to say that such a stool signifies, but he said that it has; and yet it is the affected bodies that have the harms, while the causes produce them and the signs indicate them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁρμᾷ, σινόμορα, ἑλκῶδες

"Such an evacuation has other great harms as well (for it does not quench the burning heat from the hypochondria, but stirs it up; 662 and it produces distress and tossing of the limbs), and it is ulcerating both to the intestine and to the seat; the remedies for these will be written." It is his custom to call harms σίνεα, as was also shown in the commentary on Fractures and Joints; for indeed the Ionians say σίνειν instead of βλάπτειν ('to harm'); and he himself has also often named harmful things σινόμορα. He says, then, that the aforementioned evacuation has great harms, that is, the one that is intensely bilious and at the same time also foamy. It is often his custom, in the case of signs, to use the terms that belong to causes and to the bodies that are harmed. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ξυσματώδη

What, then, the harms are that result from the stools just mentioned, hear from him himself as he says: "for it neither quenches the burning heat from the hypochondria, but 663 rather stirs it up." He said this because often the evacuation of bilious matters immediately quenches the burning heat from the hypochondria; for this reason, then, in the case of the malignant bilious stools he added that it is not quenched but, quite the contrary, increases in those who pass such stools, and the burning heat from the hypochondria does not cease but even becomes more violent, and they toss about, that is, they have asē (restless distress) and dysphoria (discomfort), since such stools are symptoms of the fiery condition in the hypochondria; and moreover also because, in their passage through the intestines, their acridity produces a condition of scrapings (ξυσματώδη διάθεσιν). Of this latter condition, then, the intensely saturated bilious stools are causes, whereas of the condition in the hypochondria they are signs; Hippocrates, however, as I said, is accustomed sometimes to use the words that indicate causes even in the case of signs. "Remedies against these will be written."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ξυσματώδη, κατακορῆ, ὁρμᾷ

What, however, the harms are that result from the aforementioned evacuations, hear from him as he says: 'For it does not quench the burning heat from the hypochondria, but 663 stirs it up.' This he said because the discharge of bilious matter often quenches the burning heat from the hypochondria at once. For this reason, then, in the case of the malignant bilious evacuations he added that it is not quenched; on the contrary, it is wholly increased in those who pass such stools. The burning heat from the hypochondria does not cease but even becomes more violent, and they toss about, that is, they suffer asē (distress) and restless discomfort, since such evacuations are symptoms of the fiery condition in the hypochondria; and moreover because, in their passage through the intestines, their acridity produces an abraded (xysmatōdēs) condition. Of this latter condition, then, the deep-coloured bilious evacuations are causes, but of the condition in the hypochondria they are signs. Hippocrates, however, as I said, is accustomed at times to use, in speaking of signs, words that indicate causes as well. 'Remedies for these will be written.'

Greek (Kühn)

ὅτι προῄρηται καθ᾿ ἕκαστον εἶδος νοσήματος ἰάματα γράψαι καὶ διὰ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἐδήλωσε καὶ νῦν οὐχ ἧττον· εἰ δ᾿ ἔστιν Ἱπποκράτους ἄξια τὰ προσκείμενα τῷδε τῷ βιβλίῳ τὰ μετὰ τὸ λουτρὸν, 664 ὅσα τε ἐν τοῖς περὶ νόσων ἢ περὶ παθῶν γέγραπται, κατὰ τὴν ἐξήγησιν αὐτῶν ἐπισκεψόμεθα. Ἄνευ μὲν οὖν ῥοφημάτων μελικρήτῳ χρεόμενος ἀντ᾿ ἄλλου ποτοῦ ἐν ταύτῃσι τῇσι νούσοισι, πολλὰ ἂν εὐτυχοίης καὶ οὐκ ἂν πολλὰ ἀτυχοίης· οἷσι δὲ δοτέον καὶ οἷσιν οὐ δοτέον, τὰ μέγιστα εἴρηται, καὶ δι᾿ ἃ οὐ δοτέον. Ἐδήλωσε καὶ νῦν σαφέστατα, μέχρι τοῦ κριθῆναι τὸ νόσημα, μελικράτῳ μόνῳ χρῆσθαι συμβουλεύων. ἐν μόνοις γὰρ τοῖς συντηκτικοῖς νοσήμασιν, ἢ ἐφ᾿ ὧν ἀνθρώπων ἀδύνατόν ἐστιν ἐξαρκέσαι τὴν δύναμιν ἄχρι τῆς κρίσεως, ἐπὶ τὸν χυλὸν ἀφιξόμεθα· τὰ δ᾿ ἄλλα βέλτιον ἐπὶ μελικράτου μόνου διαιτᾷν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

That he has undertaken to write remedies for each kind of disease he made clear both through what preceded and now no less; but whether the things appended to this book, those after the bath, 664 and all that has been written in the works On Diseases or On Affections, are worthy of Hippocrates, we shall examine in the course of the exegesis of them. "So then, using melikraton without gruels in place of another drink in these diseases, you would have good fortune in many cases and would not have ill fortune in many; to whom it should be given and to whom it should not be given, the most important points have been stated, and the reasons why it should not be given." He has now too made it most plainly clear, advising the use of melikraton alone until the disease has reached its crisis. For only in colliquative diseases, or in those persons in whom it is impossible for the faculty to hold out until the crisis, shall we have recourse to the juice; in the other cases it is better to regulate the regimen on melikraton alone.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τὰ μετὰ τὸ λουτρὸν, συντηκτικοῖς, χυλὸν

That he has chosen to write remedies for each kind of disease he made clear both in what came before and no less now. But whether the material appended to this book, namely what comes after the bath, 664 and all that is written in On Diseases or On Affections, is worthy of Hippocrates, we shall examine in the course of our exegesis of them. Without gruels, then, if you use melicrat instead of any other drink in these diseases, you would have much success and not many failures. To whom it should be given and to whom it should not be given, the most important points have been stated, and also the reasons for which it should not be given. Now too he has made it very plain, advising the use of melicrat alone until the disease has reached its crisis. For only in colliquative diseases, or in those people whose strength cannot hold out until the crisis, shall we resort to the juice; in all other cases it is better to regulate the diet with melicrat alone.

K15.665K. XV 665, ch. 3.15, 3.16 difficulty mid (2.36/100; thin 3, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

665 Κατέγνωσται δὲ μελίκρητον ὑπὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων, ὡς καταγυιοῖ τοὺς πίνοντας καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ταχυθάνατον εἶναι νενόμισται. ἐκλήθη δὲ τοῦτο διὰ τοὺς ἀποκαρτερέοντας· ἔνιοι γὰρ μελικρήτῳ μόνῳ χρέονται ποτῷ, ὡς τοιοῦδε δῆθεν ἐόντος. Οὐ ταὐτόν ἐστι καταγυιοῦσθαί τε λέγειν ὑπὸ μελικράτου καὶ μηδ᾿ ὅλως τρέφειν ἢ ὀλιγότροφον εἶναι τὸ μελίκρατον, ἀλλ᾿ ἐπεὶ τρία ταῦτα πράγματα διαφέροντα ἀλλήλων ἐστί. τὰ μὲν γὰρ καθαιροῦντα τὴν δύναμιν, ὡς αἱμοῤῥαγία καὶ διαχώρησις πολλὴ καὶ ἱδρὼς ἄμετρός τε καὶ κατά τι συμβεβηκὸς ἀσιτία, καταγυιοῦν εἰκότως ἂν λέγοιτο· τὰ δὲ μηδ᾿ ὅλως τρέφοντα, καθάπερ τὸ ὕδωρ οὔτε καταγυιοῦν, οὔτε ῥωννύναι τὴν δύναμιν ἀληθῶς ἄν τις εἴποι, πλὴν κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἐνίοτε· τὸ γὰρ ὕδωρ οὐχ ὡς τροφὴ τὴν δύναμιν ἀνακτᾶται τῶν καμνόντων, ἀλλ᾿ ὡς φάρμακον εἰς συμμετρίαν κράσεως ἄγον τὰ διὰ τὴν ἀμετρίαν αὐτῆς ἀῤῥωστοῦντα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀλλ᾿ ἐπεὶ, ἐκλήθη δὲ τοῦτο

665 Melikraton has been condemned by people, on the grounds that it enfeebles those who drink it, and for this reason it has come to be regarded as bringing a swift death. It acquired this reputation because of those who starve themselves to death; for some people use melikraton alone as their drink, as though it were indeed of such a kind. It is not the same thing to say that one is enfeebled by melikraton and to say that melikraton does not nourish at all or is of little nourishment; but [this is so] since these are three things differing from one another. For the things that pull down the faculty, such as haemorrhage and copious evacuation and immoderate sweat and, in some accidental way, abstinence from food, might reasonably be said to enfeeble; whereas of things that do not nourish at all, such as water, one could not truly say either that they enfeeble or that they strengthen the faculty, except sometimes accidentally; for water restores the faculty of the sick not as nourishment but as a drug, bringing to a due proportion of mixture those things that are weak on account of its disproportion.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀλλ᾿ ἐπεὶ, ἐκλήθη, ταχυθάνατον, κατά τι συμβεβηκὸς

665 Melicrat has been condemned by people on the grounds that it enfeebles those who drink it, and for this reason it has been held to bring a speedy death. It was so called on account of those who starve themselves to death; for some people use melicrat alone as their drink, on the supposition, of course, that it is of such a nature. It is not the same thing to say that one is enfeebled by melicrat and that melicrat does not nourish at all or is of little nourishment; rather, since these are three things differing from one another. For the things that bring down the strength, such as haemorrhage, copious evacuation, immoderate sweat and, in some incidental respect, abstinence from food, might reasonably be said to enfeeble; but the things that do not nourish at all, such as water, one could not truly say either to enfeeble or to strengthen the strength, except at times incidentally. For water restores the strength of patients not as food, but as a drug, bringing into due proportion of mixture those parts that are weak through its disproportion.

Greek (Kühn)

666 τρίτα πρὸς τούτοις τὰ βραχέως τρέφοντα· καὶ δύναιτο μὲν ἄν τις μόνοις αὐτοῖς χρώμενος ἀποθανεῖν, οὐ μὴν ὡς καθαιροῦσι τὴν δύναμιν, ἀλλ᾿ ὡς οὐχ ἱκανῶς ῥωννύουσιν. ἐκ τούτων οὖν ἐστι καὶ τὸ μελίκρατον· εἰ γάρ τις ἐν τέσσαρσιν ἡμέραις ἀσιτήσας ἀποθνήσκειν μέλλῃ, δύναιτ᾿ ἂν οὗτος μελικράτῳ χρώμενος οὐ μόνον εἰς τὴν ε΄, ἀλλὰ καὶ εἰς τὴν στ΄ ἀφικέσθαι. ὁ δ᾿ αὐτὸς λόγος καὶ περὶ τοῦ μέλιτος· ἡ γὰρ τούτου φύσις ἐστὶν ἡ καὶ τὸ μελίκρατον ἐκ τῶν βραχέως τρεφόντων ἐργαζομένη· τὴν γὰρ ὑγρασίαν τῆς τροφῆς ὄχημα καὶ οὐ τροφὴν οὐ μόνος ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, ἀλλὰ καὶ σχεδὸν ἅπαντες οἱ ἄριστοι τῶν ἰατρῶν ἀπεφήναντο. ταῦτ᾿ οὖν αὐτὰ τὰ νῦν εἰρημένα ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἔγραψε περὶ μελικράτου καὶ μέλιτος οὐκ ἀσαφῶς διὰ τῶν ἑξῆς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

666 Third, in addition to these, are the things that nourish only slightly; and someone could indeed die using these alone, yet not because they diminish the strength, but because they do not sufficiently strengthen it. Among these, then, is melikraton too; for if someone who had fasted were going to die in four days, this person, using melikraton, could arrive not only at the fifth day but even at the sixth. The same account holds also concerning honey; for it is the nature of this that makes melikraton too one of the things that nourish only slightly; for not only Hippocrates but also nearly all the best physicians have declared that the moisture of the nourishment is a vehicle and not nourishment. These very things, then, which have just been said, Hippocrates wrote concerning melikraton and honey, not obscurely, in what follows.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθαιροῦσι

666 Third, in addition to these, are the things that nourish only slightly. Someone who used these alone could indeed die, not, however, because they bring down the strength, but because they do not strengthen it sufficiently. Melicrat, then, is one of these. Greek (Kühn)

Τὸ δ᾿ οὐ παντάπασιν ὧδε ἔχει, ἀλλ᾿ ὕδατος μὲν 667 πολλῷ ἰσχυρότερόν ἐστι πινόμενον μοῦνον, ἢν μὴ ἐκταράσσῃ τὴν κοιλίην. Οὐ γὰρ παντάπασιν ἀληθές ἐστι τὸ διὰ τὸ μελίκρατον ἀποθνήσκειν τοὺς ἀποκαρτεροῦντας· ἀπέθανον γὰρ ἂν, εἰ καὶ μόνον ὕδωρ ἔπινον, ἀλλ᾿ ὅτι βραχεῖαν τροφὴν δίδωσι τῷ σώματι, διὰ τοῦτο μόνον μὲν ὂν οὐχ ἱκανόν ἐστι διαφυλάττειν τὴν ζωὴν, ὥσπερ καὶ χυλῷ πτισάνης, εἴ τις ἐθέλοι ῥοφεῖν μόνης εἰσαεί. ἀλλ᾿ ὕδατος μὲν πολλῷ ἰσχυρότερόν ἐστι πινόμενον μοῦνον, εἰ μὴ ἐκταράσσει τὴν κοιλίην. ἐνδείκνυσθαι δοκεῖ διὰ τῆς ἰσχυρότερον φωνῆς ὅτι ἔχειν τι καὶ τὸ ὕδωρ δύναται εἰς τὸ τρέφειν· ἐγχωρεῖ δὲ διὰ τὸ τὴν ξηρότητα τοῦ σώματος τῆς γαστρὸς ἐπιτέγγεσθαι, διαρκεῖν ἐπὶ πλεῖον τοὺς πίνοντας ὕδωρ τῶν μηδ᾿ ὅλως προσφερομένων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

"But the matter is not altogether thus; rather, it is 667 much stronger than water when drunk alone, unless it disturbs the belly." For it is not altogether true that those who starve themselves to death die because of the melikraton; for they would have died even if they drank only water. But because it gives little nourishment to the body, for this reason, when it is alone, it is not sufficient to preserve life, just as is the case also with the juice of ptisan, if someone should wish to sip it alone continually. But it is much stronger than water when drunk alone, if it does not disturb the belly. He seems to indicate, by the word "stronger", that water too can have some capacity for nourishing; and it is possible that, because the dryness of the body of the stomach is moistened, those who drink water hold out longer than those who take nothing at all.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χυλῷ πτισάνης ... μόνης (the dative χυλῷ and the genitive μόνης agreeing with πτισάνης are awkward; perhaps read μόνῳ), τὴν ξηρότητα τοῦ σώματος τῆς γαστρὸς (either 'the dryness of the body of the stomach' or 'of the body and of the stomach'), ἀποκαρτεροῦντας (rendered 'those who starve themselves')

But this is not entirely so; rather, drunk alone it is much 667 stronger than water, unless it upsets the bowel. For it is not entirely true that those who starve themselves die because of the melicrat; for they would have died even if they had drunk only water. Rather, because it gives scant nourishment to the body, for this reason, taken alone, it is not sufficient to preserve life, just as is the case also with the juice of ptisane, if someone should wish to sip it alone for ever. 'But drunk alone it is much stronger than water, unless it upsets the bowel.' By the word 'stronger' he seems to indicate that water too can contribute something towards nourishing; and it is possible that, because the dryness of the body of the stomach is moistened, those who drink water hold out longer than those who take in nothing at all.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀσφαλῶς δὲ προσέθηκεν, εἰ μὴ ταράσσει τὴν κοιλίαν, ἐπὶ τοῦ μελικράτου τὸν λόγον ποιούμενος· ἐκείνους γὰρ δεόντως καὶ χείρονας ἑαυτῶν ἐργάζεται διὰ τὴν κένωσιν, οἶς ταράσσει τὴν κοιλίαν· ἅμα καὶ τοῦ 668 ταράττειν ῥήματος οὐ τὴν σύμμετρον ὑποχώρησιν, ἀλλὰ τὴν πλεονάζουσαν δηλοῦντος. εἰδέναι μέντοι χρὴ τὸ μὲν ὠμὸν μελίκρατον ὑπακτικώτερον εἶναι, τὸ δὲ καλῶς ἡψημένον τροφιμώτερον· ἀλλὰ τοῦτο μὲν ἐγνώσθω σοι κατὰ τὸ πάρεργον, ἴδωμεν δὲ ἃ ἑξῆς γράφει παραβάλλων οἴνῳ μελίκρατον. Ἀτὰρ καὶ οἴνου λευκοῦ καὶ λεπτοῦ καὶ ὀλιγοφόρου καὶ ἀνόσμου ᾗ μὲν ἰσχυρότερόν ἐστιν, ᾗ δὲ ἀσθενέστερον. Ἔνιοι χωρὶς τοῦ ἐστὶ γράφουσι τὴν ῥῆσιν οὕτως· ἀτὰρ καὶ οἴνου λεπτοῦ καὶ ὀλιγοφόρου καὶ ἀνόσμου ᾗ μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, ᾗ δὲ ἀσθενέστερον. ἔνιοι δὲ ἀντὶ τοῦ ᾗ τὸ ἐνίῃ γράφουσιν οὕτως· ἀτὰρ καὶ οἴνου ὀλιγοφόρου καὶ λεπτοῦ καὶ ἀνόσμου ἐνίῃ μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, ἐνίῃ δὲ ἀσθενέστερον. ἐξ ἁπάντων γε μὴν ἡ αὐτὴ δηλοῦται δύναμις καὶ ἔννοια, κατά τι μὲν λέγοντος ἰσχυρότερον εἶναι τὸ μελίκρατον τοῦ τοιούτου οἴνου, κατά τι δὲ ἀσθενέστερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And he safely added "if it does not disturb the belly", making his statement about melikraton; for it rightly makes those persons even worse than they were, by reason of the emptying, in whom it disturbs the belly—at the same time, the word 668 "disturb" indicates not the moderate evacuation but the excessive one. One must know, however, that raw melikraton is more laxative, while that which has been well boiled is more nourishing; but let this be known to you as a side-point, and let us look at what he writes next, comparing melikraton with wine. "But also in relation to wine that is white and thin and bears little water and is odourless, in one respect it is stronger, in another weaker." Some write the passage without the "is", thus: "but also in relation to wine that is thin and bears little water and is odourless, in one respect stronger, in another weaker." Others write ἐνίῃ instead of ᾗ, thus: "but also in relation to wine that bears little water and is thin and odourless, in one respect (ἐνίῃ) stronger, in one respect weaker." From all of them, nonetheless, the same force and meaning is indicated, since he says that melikraton is in some respect stronger than such a wine, and in some respect weaker.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οἶς (read as οἷς), δεόντως, ὑπακτικώτερον

He added, with good reason, 'if it does not disturb the belly' when he was speaking about melicrat; for in those whose belly it disturbs, it naturally makes them worse than they were, because of the evacuation. At the same time the word 'to disturb' 668 indicates not the moderate discharge but the excessive one. One must know, however, that raw melicrat is more laxative, whereas well-boiled melicrat is more nourishing. But let this be known to you as a side-remark; let us see what he writes next, comparing melicrat with wine. 'Moreover, compared with white, thin, oligophoros (of little bearing) and odourless wine, it is in one respect stronger, in another weaker.' Some people write the phrase without the 'is', thus: 'Moreover, compared with thin, oligophoros and odourless wine, in one respect stronger, in another weaker.' Others write 'in some respects' (eniēi) instead of 'in one respect' (hēi), thus: 'Moreover, compared with oligophoros, thin and odourless wine, in some respects stronger, in some respects weaker.' From all of them, however, the same meaning and sense is made clear: he is saying that melicrat is in some respect stronger than such a wine, and in some respect weaker.

K15.668-669K. XV 668–669, ch. 3.18 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐγὼ μὲν οὖν 669 ἐπεμνήσθην οἴνου τοιούτου κατὰ τὸν περὶ τῶν οἴνων λόγον· ὁ δ᾿ Ἱπποκράτης αὐτὸν ὥσπερ οὐκ εἰδὼς ἐσιώπησεν, ἀλλὰ νῦν γε σαφῶς ἐδίδαξε τήν τε σύστασιν αὐτοῦ μηνύσας ἐκ τοῦ φάναι λεπτοῦ, καὶ τὴν τῆς δυνάμεως ἀσθένειαν ἐκ τοῦ προσγράψαι καὶ ὀλιγοφόρου· καλεῖται γὰρ οὕτως ὁ τῆς ὕδατος μίξεως ὀλιγίστης χρῄζων εἰς τὸ ποθῆναι. κατὰ τοὐναντίον γὰρ ὁ πολλοῦ δεόμενος ὕδατος εἰς εὐκρασίαν ὀνομάζεται πολυφόρος, ἐν ἴσῳ τῷ πολὺ φέρειν ὕδατος δεόμενος εἰς τὴν εὔκρατον μίξιν· ὁ δ᾿ ὀλιγοφόρος οἶνος ἔκλυτός τε καὶ ἀσθενὴς καὶ ἄχρηστος εἰς ποτὸν γίνεται, εἰ πλέονι κεράσεις ὕδατι. καὶ μὴν καὶ τὸ ἄοδμον αὐτοῦ μέγιστον γνώρισμα τῆς ἀσθενείας τε καὶ ψυχρότητός ἐστιν, ὕδατι καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο ἐοικὼς, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τῶν ἰσχυρὰν ἐχόντων ὀσμὴν μέγιστον τοῦτ᾿ ἔστι τῆς ῥώμης σημεῖον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

I myself, then, 669 made mention of such a wine in my discourse on wines; but Hippocrates kept silent about it as though he did not know it, yet now at any rate he has taught it clearly, indicating its consistency by saying "thin," and the weakness of its power by adding "and bearing little water"; for that wine is so called which requires the least admixture of water in order to be drunk. For, conversely, the wine that needs much water for a good blend is named "bearing much water," which is the same as saying that it needs to bear much water for the well-blended mixture; whereas the wine bearing little water becomes slack and weak and useless for drink if you mix it with more water. And indeed its being without odour too is the greatest token of its weakness and coldness, since in this respect also it resembles water, just as, in the case of wines that have a strong smell, this is the greatest sign of their strength.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

Now I 669 made mention of such a wine in my account concerning wines; Hippocrates, however, passed over it in silence, as though he did not know it. But now at least he has taught it clearly, indicating both its consistency, by saying 'thin', and the weakness of its power, by adding 'and of little bearing (oligophoros)'. For that wine is so called which requires the smallest admixture of water in order to be drunk. Conversely, the wine that needs much water to reach a good temperament (eukrasia) is named 'of much bearing' (polyphoros), which is as much as to say 'needing to bear much water to make the well-tempered mixture'. The wine of little bearing, by contrast, becomes slack, weak and useless for drinking if you mix it with more water. Moreover, its being odourless is the greatest mark of its weakness and coldness, since in this respect too it resembles water; just as, among wines that have a strong smell, this is the greatest sign of their strength.

K15.669-670K. XV 669–670, ch. 3.18, 3.19 difficulty high (5.04/100; thin 6, absent 0) (a) fail (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ὁ μὲν γὰρ ὑδατώδης οἶνος ὀλιγίστην μὲν ἐν τῷ ξύλῳ τῆς ἀμπέλου μεταβολὴν ἔσχηκεν, ὀλιγίστην δὲ καὶ τοῦ γεώδους στοιχείου τὴν μίξιν, ὥστε ὕδωρ εἶναι, βραχὺ γεώδους τε 670 καὶ θερμῆς οὐσίας προσειληφὸς, ἢ μετειληφὸς, εἴς τε τὴν τούτων ἕνωσιν ὀλιγίστης κατεργασίας δεηθείς. ὁ δ᾿ ἐναντίος οἰνώδης τε καὶ πολυφόρος οὔθ᾿ ὑπὸ βραχείας κατεργασίας γίγνεται τοιοῦτος, ὡς ἂν ἐπὶ πλεῖστον ἀφιστάμενος ὕδατος, ἀξιόλογον δὲ διὰ τοῦτ᾿ αὐτὸ τὴν θερμασίαν καὶ τὴν ὀδμὴν ἐπικτᾶται. Μέγα μὴν διαφέρει καὶ οἴνου καὶ μέλιτος ἀκρητότης ἐς ἰσχὺν ἀμφοῖν. ὅμως τοίνυν εἰ καὶ διπλάσιον μέτρον οἴνου ἀκρήτου πίνοι τις ἢ ὁκόσον μέλι ἐκλείχοι, πολλῷ ἂν δήπου ἰσχυρότερος εἴη ὑπὸ τοῦ μέλιτος, μοῦνον εἰ μὴ ταράσσοι τὴν κοιλίην· πολλαπλάσιον γὰρ καὶ τὸ κόπριον διεξίοι ἂν αὐτέῳ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For the watery wine has undergone the least change in the wood of the vine, and has also the least admixture of the earthy element, so that it is water which has taken on in addition, or taken a share of, a small amount of earthy and 670 hot substance, and has required very little elaboration for the union of these. But the opposite wine, vinous and able to bear much water, does not become such through a small elaboration, inasmuch as it departs very far from water, and for this very reason it acquires in addition a considerable heat and odour. Indeed the unmixedness of both wine and honey makes a great difference for the strength of both. Nevertheless, even if one were to drink a double measure of unmixed wine compared with the amount of honey one licked up, one would surely be much stronger from the honey, provided only that it did not disturb the belly; for many times as much excrement, too, would pass through him.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὥστε ὕδωρ εἶναι, προσειληφὸς, ἢ μετειληφὸς, πολυφόρος, ἐς ἰσχὺν ἀμφοῖν, ὅμως τοίνυν

For the watery wine has undergone very little change in the wood of the vine, and has also had very little admixture of the earthy element, so that it is water that has taken in, or received a share of, a little earthy 670 and hot substance, and has needed very little elaboration for the union of these. The opposite wine, vinous and 'polyphoros' (one that bears much water), does not become such through brief elaboration, inasmuch as it stands at the greatest remove from water, and for this very reason it acquires considerable heat and smell. Indeed, the unmixedness both of wine and of honey makes a great difference as regards the strength of both. Nevertheless, even if someone were to drink a measure of unmixed wine double the quantity of honey he licks up, he would surely be much stronger from the honey, provided only that it did not disturb the bowel; for the excrement too would pass through him in many times greater quantity.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

If 'melikraton' had been said and not 'honey', it would have been clearer; for his discussion up to this point was about melikraton, and he compared this with wine that has been mixed, evidently with 671 water, not with the thing itself by itself; but in order that the comparison might be fair, for this reason he brought the discussion back to wine itself and honey. Some people, in comparisons of this kind, reason fallaciously, not only against others willingly, but also against themselves unwillingly; and some, having at any rate made trial of certain less diluted mixtures, attach the power observed in those not only to unmixed wines alone, but also to wines mixed in whatever manner; just as, conversely, others, having made trial of more watery mixtures, have pronounced on the basis of those about all wines mixed in whatever manner.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀκρατεστέρων

If "of melicrat" had been said and not "of honey", it would have been clearer; for his discussion up to this point was about melicrat, and he compared this with wine that was mixed, namely with 671 water, not taken by itself. But so that the comparison might be a fair one, he for this reason brought the discussion back to the wine itself and the honey itself. In comparisons of this kind some people reason fallaciously, misleading not only others willingly but also themselves unwillingly. Some, having at least had experience of certain less diluted wines, attach the power they observed in those not only to the unmixed alone but also to wines mixed in any way whatever; just as, again, others, having had experience of more watery ones, have pronounced on the basis of those about all wines mixed in any way whatever.

K15.671-672K. XV 671–672, ch. 3.19 difficulty low (0.51/100; thin 0, absent 1) (a) fail (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

οὐ μὴν οὕτως ἐχρῆν ποιεῖσθαι τὰς κρίσεις τῶν πραγμάτων, ἀλλὰ λογίσασθαι δεῖν ἀνάλογον τὸ μελίκρατον ἐν τῇ κράσει τῶν πινομένων οἴνων κατὰ τὴν τοῦ ὕδατος μίξιν, ὡς μὴ τὸν οἶνον ἀκρατέστερον εἶναι, τὸ δὲ μελίκρατον ὑδαρέστερον, εἰ μέλλεις δικαίαν ποιεῖσθαι τὴν παραβολὴν αὐτῶν· ἐπεὶ δὲ χαλεπόν ἐστι τὴν ἀναλογίαν ἀκριβῶς ἐν τῇ μίξει φυλάξαι, διὰ τοῦτο ἐπ᾿ αὐτὰ τὰ ὄντως ἄμικτα καὶ κυρίως ἄκρατα καλούμενα μετήνεγκε τὸν λόγον ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, οἴνῳ μηδ᾿ ὅλως κεκραμένῳ παραβάλλων τὸ μέλι· καί φησι διπλάσιον οἶνον 672 πινόμενον ἧττον τρέφειν ἁπλοῦ πλήθους μέλιτος. ἐπεὶ δὲ πλείω γένη τῶν οἴνων εἰσὶν, οὐ τοὺς ἰσχυροὺς καὶ κεφαλὴν πλῆξαι δυναμένους καὶ παραφροσύνας ἐργάσασθαι προεχειρίσατο κατὰ τὸν λόγον, ἀλλὰ τὸν ἀσθενῆ καὶ ὑδατώδη καὶ τούτῳ παραβάλλων τὸ μέλι τροφιμώτερον ἀπέδειξεν. ὅταν δὲ διπλασίῳ ὄγκῳ οἴνου ἀξιώσῃ μέλιτος ἥμισυ παραβάλλειν, οὐ τοῦτό φησιν, ὅτι μόνον ἑκάτερον ὁ κάμνων αὐτὸ καθ᾿ ἑαυτὸ προσφερέσθω, μὴ μιγνὺς ὕδωρ· ἀλλ᾿ εἰ μετὰ τὸ μετρῆσαι τὸ πλῆθος ἑκατέρου προσφέροιτο καθ᾿ ὅ τι βούλοιτο, διαφέρει οὐδὲν ἧττον. δύναται γὰρ εἰ τύχοι κύαθον ἕνα μέλιτος ἢ καθ᾿ ἑαυτὸν λαμβάνειν ἢ ὅσον βούλεται μιγνὺς ὕδατι προσφέρεσθαι καὶ τοῦτο καθ᾿ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν πιεῖν· ἢ πάλιν οἴνου κυάθους δύο ἑκάστης ἡμέρας ὡς αὐτὸς βούλεται κεραννὺς ὕδατι προσφέρεσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διαφέρει οὐδὲν ἧττον

Yet one ought not to have made one's judgements of the matters in this way, but one ought to reckon the melikraton as proportionate, in its blending, to the wines that are drunk, in respect of the admixture of water, so that the wine is not the less diluted and the melikraton the more watery, if you are going to make a fair comparison of them. But since it is difficult to preserve the proportion exactly in the mixing, for this reason Hippocrates transferred the discussion to the things themselves that are really unmixed and properly called unmixed (ἄκρατα), comparing honey with wine not diluted at all; and he says that a double quantity of wine 672 when drunk nourishes less than a single quantity of honey. And since there are several kinds of wines, he did not take up in his discussion the strong ones, which are able to strike the head and produce derangements (παραφροσύναι), but the weak and watery one, and comparing honey with this he showed it to be more nourishing. And when he thinks it right to compare half the quantity of honey with a double bulk of wine, he is not saying this, that the patient should take each of them only by itself, without mixing in water; rather, if, after measuring out the quantity of each, he were to take it in whatever manner he wished, it makes no difference all the same. For he can, say, take one kyathos of honey either by itself or, mixing in as much water as he wishes, take it so and drink this each day; or again take two kyathoi of wine each day, diluting it with water as he himself wishes.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαφέρει οὐδὲν ἧττον, λογίσασθαι δεῖν ἀνάλογον τὸ μελίκρατον

However, one ought not to make one's judgements of the matters in this way. Rather, one must reckon that the honey-water has to be proportionate, in its mixture, to the wines that are drunk, with respect to the admixture of water, so that the wine is not less diluted while the honey-water is more watery, if you are going to make a fair comparison of them. But since it is difficult to preserve the proportion exactly in the mixing, for this reason Hippocrates transferred the argument to the things themselves that are truly unmixed and properly called neat, comparing honey with wine not mixed at all. And he says that wine drunk in double quantity 672 nourishes less than a single amount of honey. And since there are several kinds of wines, he did not select in his argument the strong ones, those able to strike the head and to produce deliriums, but the weak and watery one; and by comparing honey with this he showed honey to be more nourishing. When he thinks fit to compare half as much honey with a double volume of wine, he does not mean that the patient should take each of the two only by itself, without mixing in water. Rather, if after the quantity of each has been measured it is administered in whatever way he wishes, the difference holds none the less. For he can, for example, take one cyathus of honey either by itself, or mixed with as much water as he wishes, and drink this each day; or again take two cyathi of wine each day, mixed with water as he himself wishes.

Greek (Kühn)

γνωσθήσεται γὰρ ἐκ τῆς τοιαύτης πείρας ἐπὶ πλέον τῶν ἀνθρώπων ὁμοίως ἐχόντων κατ᾿ ἄλλα καὶ μέντοι καὶ μᾶλλον ἐφ᾿ ἑνὸς, οὐ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν χρόνον, ἡ ἑκατέρου δύναμις ἀκριβής· εἰ γὰρ μὴ κατὰ τινα φύσεως ἰδιότητα ταράττοι τὴν γαστέρα τὸ μέλι, 673 πλείοσιν ἡμέραις ἐξαρκέσουσιν οἱ τῷ μέλιτι χρώμενοι. διὰ τί οὖν, εἴπερ τοῦτο οὕτως ἔχει, κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆς ῥήσεως εἶπεν· ἀτὰρ καὶ οἴνου λεπτοῦ καὶ ὀλιγοφόρου καὶ ἀνόσμου ἐνίῃ μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, ἐνίῃ δὲ ἀσθενέστερον εἶναι τὸ μελίκρητον; οὐ γὰρ ἐνίοτε μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, ἐνίοτε δὲ ἀσθενέστερον, ἀλλ᾿ ἰσχυρότερον ἀεὶ φαίνεται κατὰ τὸν εἰρημένον ἄρτι λόγον. ὅτι πρὸ τοῦ διορισθῆναι καὶ διδαχθῆναι πρὸς αὐτοῦ περὶ τῆς δικαίας παραβολῆς ἀδιορίστως τοῦ λόγου περαινομένου καλῶς καὶ ἀσφαλῶς εἶπεν, εὑρεθήσεται τὸ μελίκρατον τοῦ οἴνου κατά τι μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, κατά τι δὲ ἀσθενέστερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For from such a trial the precise power of each will come to be known, on a larger number of people who are in a similar condition in other respects, and indeed still more on a single person, though not at the same time; for if the honey does not disturb the belly on account of some peculiarity of nature, 673 those who use the honey will hold out for more days. Why then, if this is so, did he say at the beginning of the passage: "But also, as compared with wine that is thin, bears little water and is odourless, melikreton is in one respect stronger and in another weaker"? For it does not appear sometimes stronger and sometimes weaker, but always stronger, according to the argument just stated. Because, before the matter of the just comparison had been distinguished and taught by him, the argument being carried through without distinction, he said well and safely that melikraton will be found in some respect stronger than wine, in some respect weaker.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀλιγοφόρου, ἐπὶ πλέον τῶν ἀνθρώπων, οὐ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν χρόνον, ἐξαρκέσουσιν

For from an experiment of this kind, made on a larger number of people who are alike in other respects, and indeed still more on a single person, though not at the same time, the exact power of each will be known. For unless honey disturbs the belly through some peculiarity of nature, 673 those who use honey will hold out for more days. Why then, if this is so, did he say at the beginning of the passage: 'but also, compared with a thin wine, of little bearing (oligophoros) and without smell, the melicrat is in some respects stronger, in some respects weaker'? For it does not appear sometimes stronger and sometimes weaker, but always stronger, according to the argument just stated. The answer is that before distinctions had been drawn and he had given instruction about the fair comparison, while the argument was being carried through without distinction, he rightly and safely said that the melicrat will be found in one respect stronger than wine, in another weaker.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐὰν γὰρ μὴ διορίσηταί τις ἑκατέρου τὴν ἀναλογίαν τῆς κράσεως, ἐνίοτε μὲν ἀσθενέστερος ὁ οἶνος, ἐνίοτε δὲ τὸ μελίκρατον εἶναι δόξει· διορισθέντων δὲ, ὡς εἴρηται, καὶ τῆς κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα ταραχῆς ἐπιβλεπομένης, τὸ μελίκρατον ἰσχυρότερον εἰς θρέψιν ἀεὶ φαίνεται τοῦ κεκραμένου κατὰ τὴν αὐτὴν ἀναλογίαν οἴνου· ἐὰν δὲ ὅτι καὶ τὸ κόπριον ἐκκρινόμενον ἐπὶ τῷ μελικράτῳ σύμμετρον, ὡς μὴ καταλύειν τὴν δύναμιν ἐννοήσεις, ἔτι καὶ μᾶλλόν σοι φανεῖται 674 πλείων ἡ ἐκ μελικράτου θρέψις ἤπερ ἡ ἐξ οἴνου γιγνομένη. εἰ γὰρ καὶ κόπρου πολλαπλασίου διεξιόντος, ὅμως ὁ κάμνων ἐξαρκεῖ πλείοσιν ἡμέραις ἐπὶ τῷ μελικράτῳ, δυνατώτερον δηλονότι πρὸς τὴν θρέψιν οἴνου φαίνεται τὸ μέλι. Εἰ μέντοι ῥοφήματι χρέοιτο πτισάνῃ, ἐπιπίνοι δὲ μελίκρητον, ἄγαν πλησμονῶδες ἂν εἴη καὶ φυσῶδες καὶ τοῖσι κατὰ τὰ ὑποχόνδρια σπλάγχνοισιν ἀσύμφορον· προπινόμενον μέντοι πρὸ ῥοφημάτων μελίκρητον οὐ βλάπτει ὡς ἐπιπινόμενον, ἀλλὰ τι καὶ ὠφελέει. Περὶ τῆς τάξεως ὁ λόγος αὐτῷ, ᾗ κάλλιστ᾿ ἄν τις χρῷτο τῷ μελικράτῳ τε καὶ πτισάνῃ. κοινὸς δὲ ἔστω σοι λόγος καὶ οὗτος, ὡς ἐπὶ παραδείγματος ἑνὸς διδασκόμενος μελικράτου τε καὶ πτισάνης· ἀεὶ γὰρ χρὴ πρότερον μὲν προσφέρεσθαι τά τε ῥᾳδίως διαφθειρόμενα καὶ μᾶλλον ὑπιόντα, δεύτερα δὲ τὰ ἐναντία.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For if one does not define the proportion of the mixture of each, sometimes the wine will seem to be the weaker, sometimes the melikraton; but once these have been defined, as has been said, and the disturbance of the belly is also taken into consideration, the melikraton always appears stronger for nourishment than wine mixed in the same proportion. And if you also bear in mind that the excrement evacuated on melikraton is moderate, so as not to break down the faculty, the nourishment from melikraton will appear to you still more 674 to be greater than that arising from wine. For if, even when many times as much excrement passes through, the patient nevertheless holds out for more days on melikraton, honey clearly appears more powerful than wine for nourishment. If, however, one should use ptisan as a gruel, and drink melikraton after it, it would be exceedingly filling and flatulent and unsuitable for the viscera in the hypochondria; melikraton drunk beforehand, before the gruels, however, does not harm as it does when drunk afterwards, but even helps somewhat. His discussion concerns the order in which one would best use melikraton and ptisan. And let this too be for you a general account, taught as on a single example, that of melikraton and ptisan: for one must always administer first the things that are easily corrupted and pass down more readily, and second their opposites.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κόπρου πολλαπλασίου διεξιόντος (sits awkwardly with the preceding κόπριον ... σύμμετρον; translated as transmitted), διαφθειρόμενα

For if one does not determine the proportion of the mixture of each, sometimes the wine will seem to be the weaker, sometimes the melicrat; but when these have been determined, as has been said, and the disturbance of the belly is also taken into account, melicrat always appears stronger for nourishment than wine mixed in the same proportion. And if you also consider that the excrement evacuated upon melicrat is moderate, so as not to dissolve the strength, it will appear to you still more that 674 the nourishment from melicrat is greater than that which comes from wine. For if, even though many times more excrement passes through, the patient nevertheless holds out for more days on melicrat, honey evidently appears more powerful for nourishment than wine. If, however, one were to use ptisan as a gruel, and drink melicrat after it, it would be excessively filling and flatulent and disadvantageous to the viscera in the hypochondria; melicrat drunk beforehand, before the gruels, however, does not harm as it does when drunk afterwards, but even benefits somewhat. His discussion concerns the order in which one may best use both melicrat and ptisan. Let this account too be a general one for you, as taught on a single example, that of melicrat and ptisan: for one must always administer first the things that are easily corrupted and pass downwards more readily, and second their opposites.

Greek (Kühn)

πτισάνη δὲ μελικράτου δυσφθαρτότερον 675 καὶ ὑπέρχεται βράδιον. πρώτου μὲν οὖν τοῦ μελικράτου ληφθέντος οἷον ὑφήγησίς τις ἔξ αὐτοῦ γένοιτ᾿ ἂν τῇ πτισάνῃ τῆς διαχωρήσεως· εἰ δὲ πτισάνη πρώτη ληφθείη, κωλυόμενον ὑπιέναι τὸ μελίκρατον ἐμφυσήσει τε τὸ ὑποχόνδριον εἴς τε τὴν χολὴν μεταβληθὲν ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ καὶ τῇ πτισάνῃ τι λυμανεῖται. Ἑφθὸν δὲ μελίκρητον ἐσιδεῖν μὲν πολλῷ κάλλιον τοῦ ὠμοῦ· λαμπρὸν γὰρ καὶ λεπτὸν καὶ λευκὸν καὶ διαφανὲς γίγνεται· ἀρετὴν δὲ ἥντινα αὐτέῳ προσθέω διαφέρουσάν τι τοῦ ὠμοῦ οὐκ ἔχω. οὔτε γὰρ ἥδιόν ἐστι τοῦ ὠμοῦ, ἢν τυγχάνοι γε τὸ μέλι καλὸν ἐόν· ἀσθενέστερον μέντοι γε τοῦ ὠμοῦ καὶ ἀκοπρωδέστερόν ἐστιν, ὧν οὐδετέρου ἐς τιμωρίην προσδέεται μελίκρητον. ἄγχιστα δὲ χρηστέον αὐτῷ τοιῷδε ἐόντι, ἢν τὸ μέλι τυγχάνοι πονηρὸν ἐὸν καὶ ἀκάθαρτον καὶ μέλαν καὶ μὴ εὐῶδες· ἀφέλοιτο γὰρ ἂν ἡ ἕψησις τῶν κακοτήτων αὐτοῦ τὰ πλέονα τοῦ αἴσχεος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀκοπρωδέστερον, ἐς τιμωρίην, ἄγχιστα

Ptisan is harder to corrupt than melikraton 675 and passes down more slowly. So if the melikraton is taken first, there would arise from it, as it were, a kind of leading of the way for the passage of the ptisan; but if the ptisan were taken first, the melikraton, being prevented from passing down, will both inflate the hypochondrium and, having been changed into bile in the course of time, will also do some harm to the ptisan. Boiled melikraton is much finer to look at than raw, for it becomes bright and thin and white and transparent; but what virtue I should attribute to it that differs in any way from the raw, I do not have. For it is not more pleasant than the raw, provided at least that the honey happens to be good; it is, however, weaker than the raw and less productive of faeces (ἀκοπρωδέστερον), and melikraton has need of neither of these as an aid (ἐς τιμωρίην). One should use it in this form most especially if the honey happens to be bad and impure and dark and not fragrant; for the boiling would remove the greater part of the foulness of its bad qualities.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑφήγησίς, ἄγχιστα, τὰ πλέονα τοῦ αἴσχεος, ἐς τιμωρίην

Ptisane is harder to corrupt than honey-water 675 and passes down more slowly. If, then, the honey-water is taken first, it would provide, as it were, a kind of leading-the-way for the evacuation of the ptisane; but if the ptisane is taken first, the honey-water, being prevented from passing down, will both inflate the hypochondrium and, changed into bile in the course of time, will also do some harm to the ptisane. Boiled honey-water is much finer to look at than raw, for it becomes bright, thin, white and transparent; but what virtue I could attribute to it that differs in any way from the raw I do not have. For it is not more pleasant than the raw, if the honey happens to be good; it is, however, weaker than the raw and less productive of faeces, neither of which honey-water needs by way of help. It should be used in this form chiefly if the honey happens to be bad, impure, dark and not fragrant; for the boiling would remove the greater part of the unsightliness of its bad qualities.

Greek (Kühn)

676 Διαφορὰς ὠμοῦ τε καὶ ἑφθοῦ μελικράτου διδάσκει καθ᾿ ὅλην τήνδε τὴν ῥῆσιν, δεῖται δ᾿ ὁ λόγος οὐδεμιᾶς ἐξηγήσεως. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

676 Throughout this whole passage he teaches the differences between raw and boiled melikraton, and the account needs no exegesis. The drink called oxymel you will find to be serviceable in many ways in these diseases; for it both brings up sputum and eases breathing; it has, however, the following occasions for use. You have learned that he adds "the so-called" and "to be called" and expressions similar to these, sometimes on the ground that the appellation is not yet customary among all the Greeks, sometimes on the ground that it is used improperly, and at times on the ground that it indicates something technical, as we showed in the case of the temporal and masseter muscles. But that the appellation "oxymel" indicates nothing technical is clear to everyone; rather, it is either by way of finding fault with it, or as not yet customary, that he marked it by the addition of "so-called."

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Either is possible: for perhaps 677 it was then first coming into common use; and perhaps too he finds fault with the term, as indicating either that the drug is composed of vinegar and honey alone, or as signifying a variety of honey, as if one were to speak thus: 'sour honey has such-and-such a power.' Concerning the addition of 'so-called', then, the matter is clear; let us proceed to what is actually useful. For the composition of oxymel does not come about merely from a mixture of vinegar and honey, but from melikraton that takes on a small quantity of vinegar. And it is better that it be boiled; for the power of the vinegar is reduced by the boiling, so that the melikraton with which it is mixed receives the benefit that comes from it but does not retain the harm. Now the benefit he stated at the beginning of the passage and will state again after this; the harm he will add at the end of the whole passage. τοὺς καιροὺς ἤτοι τοὺς τῆς χρήσεως αὐτοῦ λέγει, καθότι σύνηθες, ἢ τοὺς 678 τῆς ὠφελείας, ἐπειδὴ καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο τὸ σημαινόμενον ἐδείχθη χρώμενος ἐνίοτε τῇ τοῦ καιροῦ προσηγορίᾳ. Ἐφ᾿ ὧν ἐστι χρεία τοῦ ὀξέος ὀξυμέλιτος, ἐπὶ τούτων διδάσκει τὴν ἐξ αὐτοῦ γιγνομένην ἐνίοτε βλάβην· ἐφ᾿ ὧν γὰρ ὅλως οὐδ᾿ ἂν χρήσαιτό τις ὀξεῖ τῷ τοιούτῳ φαρμάκῳ, περιττὸν τὸ καταλέγειν τὰς τοιαύτας βλάβας.

By 'occasions' he means either those of its use, as is customary, or those 678 of its benefit, since he has been shown sometimes to use the appellation 'occasion' in this signification as well. "The very acid kind would produce nothing intermediate with regard to the sputa that do not come up easily. For if it should bring up what causes rasping, and produce slipperiness, and, as it were, sweep the windpipe clean as with a feather, it would soothe the lung, for it is emollient of it; and if these things should concur, it would produce great benefit; but there are times when the very acid kind did not master the bringing up of the sputum, but rendered it more viscous and did harm." In the cases where there is need of acid oxymel, in these he teaches the harm that sometimes arises from it; for in cases where one would not at all use such a drug in acid form, it is superfluous to enumerate such harms.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐγκέρχνοντα, διαπτερώσειε, μέσον, προσεγλίσχρηνε, βρόγχον

By 'occasions' he means either those of its use, as is customary, or those 678 of its benefit, since it has been shown that he sometimes employs the term 'occasion' (kairos) in this sense as well. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔχομεν, διὰ τὸ σ, μόνῳ

For he did not even speak simply of sour oxymel, but added also 'very' (κάρτα), 679 which signifies the same as 'exceedingly' and 'strongly'. Such oxymel, then, is wont to produce nothing intermediate in the case of the thick and viscous sputa in the lung, in those cases in which one would use it on its own; but either, having cut them and scoured them off, it brings them up, or, being unable to accomplish this, it dries them. For just as such a drug is cutting, so is it also drying; and we have it (ἔχομεν) for the thick sputa in the lung not as drying (for in that way it would do harm), but as cutting and cleansing. When, therefore, it proves unable to do these things, it has done nothing useful, but has wrought the greatest harm. When, then, it fails to cleanse and to cut, he himself taught in what follows; but now I wish to speak about the reading found in many of the copies. For we find the second syllable of the word μέσον written not with σ but with ζ, so that the whole phrase is as follows: 'for what is very sour would produce nothing greater (μέζον).'

Ἢν μηδὲν ἀναπτύῃ τι διὰ πάχος καὶ γλισχρότητα τῶν πτυσμάτων, οὐ μὴν ἀσθενῆ γε τὴν δύναμιν, ὀξύμελι κελεύει δίδοσθαι τὸ λίαν ὀξὺ, κατ᾿ ὀλίγον τε καὶ ἀκροχλίαρον, ὅπερ 682 ἐστὶν ἄκρως χλιαρόν (ἄκρως δὲ ἐστι χλιαρὸν ὅπερ ἂν ᾖ μέσον ἀκριβῶς τοῦ τε ψυχροῦ καὶ τοῦ θερμοῦ)· ἰσχυρὰν γὰρ ἔχον αὐτὸ τὴν δύναμιν εὐλαβεῖται πολὺ προσφέρειν εἰς ἅπαξ. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ τὸ ψυχρὸν ἐν θέρει διδόναι, καθάπερ ἔμπροσθεν ἐκέλευσεν, εὐλαβεῖται, διὰ τὸ τὰς μὲν ἀπτύστους πτυσμάτων πλευρίτιδας καὶ περιπνευμονίας ἀπέπτους εἶναι, τῇ πέψει δ᾿ ἀεὶ τὸ μὲν θερμὸν συντελεῖν, τὸ δὲ ψυχρὸν ἐναντιοῦσθαι. τί δήποτ᾿ οὖν καὶ τὸ θερμὸν ὀξύμελι φεύγει; ἢ ὅτι διὰ τὸ κακόηθες τοῦ νοσήματος, μή πως εὐθέως ἐν τῇ τοιαύτῃ προσφορᾷ τοῦ τοιούτου πόματος, αὐτὸ μὲν τὸ πάθος ἄπεπτον ἔτι διαμένῃ, συμβῇ δὲ τὸν ἄνθρωπον ὑπὸ τοῦ θερμοῦ ὀξυμέλιτος διψωδέστερον γίνεσθαι ἑαυτοῦ; δέδεικται δ᾿ ὅτι τὸ σύμπτωμα τοῦτο γλίσχρον ἐργάζεται πτύελον. ἀσφαλέστερον οὖν αὐτῷ δοκεῖ μετρίως χλιαρὸν εἶναι τὸ ὀξύμελι, φεύγοντι τοῦ θερμοῦ τὴν ἐνδεχομένην ἀκολουθῆσαι βλάβην, τοῦ δὲ ψυχροῦ τὴν ἔξ ἀνάγκης ἑπομένην.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀκροχλίαρον

If someone brings up nothing at all because of the thickness and viscosity of the sputa, yet does not have his power weak, he orders that the very sharp oxymel be given, a little at a time and ἀκροχλίαρον, which 682 is lukewarm to the exact degree (and that is lukewarm to the exact degree which is precisely midway between the cold and the hot); for since it has a strong power, he is cautious about administering much of it at once. Likewise he is also cautious about giving it cold in summer, as he ordered earlier, because pleurisies and peripneumonias in which the sputa are not expectorated are unconcocted, and the hot always contributes to concoction while the cold opposes it. Why, then, does he also avoid hot oxymel? Is it that, because of the malignancy of the disease, he fears lest at once, upon such an administration of such a drink, the affection itself still remain unconcocted, while it happens that the man becomes more thirsty than he was owing to the hot oxymel? And it has been shown that this symptom produces viscous sputum. It therefore seems safer to him that the oxymel be moderately lukewarm, as he avoids the harm which may possibly follow from the hot, and the harm which follows of necessity from the cold.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μηδὲν ἀναπτύῃ τι, μή πως εὐθέως, ἄκρως χλιαρόν

In the same way he is also wary of giving it cold in summer, as he directed earlier, because pleurisies and peripneumonias without expectoration of sputa are unconcocted, and heat always contributes to concoction, while cold opposes it. Why then does he avoid hot oxymel as well? Is it because, on account of the malignant nature of the disease, there is a danger that, straightaway upon such an administration of such a drink, the affection itself may still remain unconcocted, while it happens that the person becomes thirstier than he was, owing to the hot oxymel? And it has been shown that this symptom produces viscous sputum. So it seems safer to him that the oxymel be moderately lukewarm, since he is avoiding the harm that may possibly follow from the hot, and that which follows of necessity from the cold.

683 Τὸ μέντοι ὀλίγον ἔποξυ ὀξύμελι ὑγραίνει μὲν τὸ στόμα καὶ φάρυγγα, ἀναγωγὸν δὲ πτυέλου ἐστὶ καὶ ἄδιψον· ὑποχονδρίῳ δὲ καὶ σπλάγχνοισι τοῖσι ταύτῃ εὐμενὲς καὶ τὰς ἀπὸ μέλιτος βλάβας ταύτῃ κωλύει· τὸ γὰρ ἐν μέλιτι χολῶδες κολάζεται. Περὶ τοῦ λίαν ὀξέος ὀξυμέλιτος συμπληρώσας τὸν λόγον ἐπὶ τὸ μετρίως ὀξὺ μετέβη κατὰ τήνδε τὴν ῥῆσιν, ὑγραίνειν αὐτὸ λέγων τὸ στόμα καὶ φάρυγγα καὶ τῶν πτυέλων ἀναγωγὸν εἶναι καὶ ἄδιψον. ἅπαντα γὰρ ἔχον ὑφ᾿ ὧν ὠφελεῖσθαι πεφύκασιν αἱ τοιαῦται νόσοι, τῶν βλαπτόντων οὐδὲν ἔχει διὰ τὴν τῆς κράσεως ἀρετήν· ἐπειδὴ τοσοῦτον τῷ μελικράτῳ μέμικται τοῦ ὄξους, ὅσον ἱκανόν ἐστι τὸ χολῶδες ἐν αὐτῷ κολάσαι, τουτέστι καθελεῖν καὶ ταπεινῶσαι· δέδεικται γάρ σοι κατὰ τὸ τέταρτον γράμμα περὶ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως ἐναντιωτάτην δύναμιν ἔχειν ὁ γλυκὺς χυμὸς τῷ ὀξεῖ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χυμὸς

683 The oxymel that is only slightly sour, however, moistens the mouth and the pharynx, and it is conducive to bringing up sputum and allays thirst; to the hypochondrium and to the viscera in that region it is kindly, and it prevents in that region the harms that come from honey; for the bilious element in honey is checked. Having completed his account of the excessively sour oxymel, he has passed on to the moderately sour kind in this passage, saying that it moistens the mouth and pharynx and is conducive to bringing up sputa and allays thirst. For possessing all the things by which such diseases are naturally benefited, it has none of the things that harm, on account of the excellence of its mixture; since just so much vinegar has been mixed with the melikraton as is sufficient to check the bilious element in it, that is, to pull it down and bring it low; for it has been shown to you in the fourth book of On the Powers of Simple Drugs that the sweet flavour (χυμός) has a power most contrary to the sour.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔποξυ, ἄδιψον, κράσεως

683 "The oxymel that is only slightly acid, however, moistens the mouth and throat, promotes the bringing up of sputum, and allays thirst; it is kindly to the hypochondrium and to the viscera in that region, and in this way it prevents the harms that come from honey; for the bilious element in honey is checked." Having completed his account of the very acid oxymel, he passed in this passage to the moderately acid kind, saying that it moistens the mouth and throat, promotes the bringing up of sputa, and allays thirst. For since it possesses everything by which such diseases are naturally benefited, it has none of the things that do harm, owing to the excellence of its mixture (krasis). So much vinegar has been mixed with the melicrat as is sufficient to check the bilious element in it, that is, to bring it down and reduce it. For it was shown to you in the fourth book On the Power of Simple Drugs that the sweet flavour has a power most contrary to the acid.

K15.683-684K. XV 683–684, ch. 3.27, 3.28 difficulty high (6.12/100; thin 3, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

τῆς οὖν τοῦ μελικράτου δυνάμεως 684 τὰ ἄλλα μὲν πάντα ἐχούσης, ὧν δεῖται τὰ τοιαῦτα νοσήματα, καθ᾿ ἓν δὲ μόνον ἐναντιουμένης αὐταῖς, ὅταν ὑπερθερμανθὲν ἐκχολωθῇ, τὴν τοιαύτην αὐτοῦ μεταβολὴν ἡ τοῦ ὄξους μίξις κωλύουσα κάλλιστον ἐργάζεται φάρμακον. ἄδιψόν τε γὰρ ἔσται καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὰ πτύελα, τά γε μὴ παντάπασι παχέα καὶ γλίσχρα, ῥᾳδίως ἀνάξει· τούτοις δ᾿ ἀμφοτέροις αὐτοῖς ἔργοις ἕπεται καὶ ἡ τῶν κατὰ τὸ στόμα καὶ τὴν φάρυγγα μορίων ὑγρότης. ᾧ δὲ λόγῳ ταῦτα δρᾷ, τούτῳ δὴ καὶ σπληνὶ καὶ ἥπατι προσφορώτατόν ἐστι, διακαθαῖρον ἀλύπως τὰ σπλάγχνα. Ἔστι δὲ καὶ φυσέων καταῤῥηκτικὸν καὶ ἐς οὔρησιν προτρεπτικόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Since, then, the power of melikraton 684 possesses all the other things that such diseases require, and is opposed to them in one respect only, namely when, having been overheated, it is turned into bile, the admixture of vinegar, by preventing such a change in it, produces a most excellent remedy. For it will be thirst-allaying, and on this account it will also readily bring up the sputa, at least those that are not altogether thick and viscous; and upon both of these very effects there follows also the moistness of the parts about the mouth and the pharynx. And by the same principle by which it does these things, it is also most suitable for the spleen and the liver, thoroughly cleansing the viscera without causing distress. It is also capable of breaking up flatulence and of promoting urination.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐταῖς (feminine plural, it does not agree with neuter νοσήματα; taken as referring to the diseases, cf. αἱ τοιαῦται νόσοι in the preceding context), ἐκχολωθῇ

Since, then, the power of melicrat 684 possesses all the other things that such diseases require, and is contrary to them in one respect only, namely when, having been overheated, it turns bilious, the admixture of vinegar, by preventing this change in it, makes a most excellent remedy. For it will be thirst-quenching, and for this reason it will also readily bring up the sputa, at least those that are not altogether thick and viscid; and both of these effects are attended by moistness of the parts in the mouth and the throat. And by the same reasoning by which it does these things, it is also most beneficial to the spleen and the liver, cleansing the viscera thoroughly and without distress. It also breaks up flatulences and promotes urination.

K15.684-685K. XV 684–685, ch. 3.28, 3.29 difficulty high (4.42/100; thin 1, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

Περὶ τοῦ μετρίως ὀξέος ὀξυμέλιτος ἔτι διαλεγόμενος εἴρηκε ταῦτα, πολλὴν δὲ τὴν χρῆσιν ἔχοντος ἐπὶ τῶν κατὰ πνεύμονα καὶ θώρακα παθῶν, ἀξιολογωτάτην τε τὴν ὠφέλειον, 685 διὰ τὴν ἔμμετρον μίξιν ὕδατός τε καὶ ὄξους καὶ μέλιτος. τὸ γὰρ τοιοῦτον ὀξύμελι, τέμνον τε τὸ παχὺ καὶ φυσῶδες ἐν ὑποχονδρίῳ πνεῦμα καὶ καταῤῥηγνύον τὰς φύσας διακαθαῖρόν τε τὰς διεξόδους πάσας, ἐπ᾿ οὖρα ποδηγεῖ τοῖς ὀῤῥώδεσί τε καὶ χολώδεσι περιττώμασιν. Ἐντέρου μέντοι τῷ κάτω μέρει πλαδαρώτερον καὶ ξύσματα ἐμποιέει. Τὴν ὑπὲρ τὸ κατὰ φύσιν ἐγγινομένην ὑγρότητα τοῖς μορίοις ἴσμεν αὐτὸν ἀεὶ πλάδον ὀνομάζοντα· τοῦτ᾿ οὖν ἐργάζεται, φησὶ, τὸ ὀξύμελι, ξύον τε τῶν ἄνωθεν ὅσον ἐμπλάττεται παχέων χυμῶν ἢ γλίσχρων, τέμνον δὲ τοῦτο καὶ ὑπάγον πρὸς τὴν ἔκκρισιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὠφέλειον

He has said these things while still discussing the moderately sour oxymel, which has extensive use in the affections of the lung and thorax, and a most notable benefit, 685 on account of the well-proportioned mixture of water, vinegar and honey. For oxymel of this kind, cutting the thick and flatulent pneuma in the hypochondrium and breaking up the flatus and thoroughly cleansing all the passages, guides the serous and bilious residues toward the urine. In the lower part of the intestine, however, it produces greater sogginess and scrapings. We know that he always calls the moisture arising in the parts beyond what is in accordance with nature plados; this, then, he says, oxymel produces, scraping away whatever of the thick or viscous humours is plastered on from the parts above, cutting this and bringing it down toward excretion.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὠφέλειον (apparently for ὠφέλειαν), πλαδαρώτερον, ποδηγεῖ τοῖς ὀῤῥώδεσί τε καὶ χολώδεσι περιττώμασιν (dative construction)

He has said these things while still discussing the moderately acid oxymel, which has a great use in the affections of the lung and thorax, and a most considerable benefit, 685 on account of the well-proportioned mixture of water, vinegar and honey. For such an oxymel, cutting the thick and flatulent pneuma in the hypochondrium, breaking up the flatulences and cleansing all the passages, leads the way to the urine for the serous and bilious residues. To the lower part of the intestine, however, it is rather sodden-making, and it produces scrapings. We know that he always calls the moisture that arises in the parts beyond what is natural 'sodden moisture' (plados). This, then, he says, the oxymel produces, scraping off from the parts above whatever thick or viscous humours are plastered on there, and cutting this and drawing it down to excretion.

Greek (Kühn)

εὐπετεῖς δὲ κατὰ τοιαύτην τὴν δύναμιν ἐργάζεται καὶ τὰς ἐξ ἥπατος ἐπαγωγὰς τῶν περιττωμάτων, ὥστε πασῶν τῶν ἄνωθεν ὑγροτήτων ἀθροιζομένων ἐν τοῖς ἐντέροις καὶ μάλιστα τοῖς κάτω, διὰ μὲν τὸ πλῆθος 686 αὐτῶν τὸν πλάδον ἐγγίνεσθαι, διὰ δὲ τὴν ποιότητα ξύεσθαι τὸ ἔντερον. καὶ γὰρ τὸ ὀξύμελι ῥυπτικὴν ἔχον δύναμιν ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ ξύσειεν ἂν τὸ ἔντερον· καὶ τὸ συγκατερχόμενον αὐτῷ χολῶδες περίττωμα τὴν αὐτὴν ἔχον δύναμιν· ὅθεν καὶ χωρὶς ὀξυμέλιτος, ἢν πλεονάσαν ποτὲ τύχῃ, ξύει τὸ ἔντερον. Ἔστι δ᾿ ὅτε καὶ φλαῦρον τοῦτο ἐν τῇσιν ὀξείῃσι νούσοισι γίνεται, μάλιστα μὲν ὅτι φῦσαν κωλύει περαιοῦσθαι, ἀλλὰ παλινδρομέειν ποιέει· ἔτι δὲ καὶ ἄλλως γυιοῖ καὶ τὰ ἀκρωτήρια ψύχει. ταύτην καὶ οἶδα μούνην βλάβην τὴν δι᾿ ὀξυμέλιτος γιγνομένην, ἥτις καὶ ἀξίη γραφῆς. Τὸ ξυσθῆναι τὸ ἔντερον οὐκ ἀγαθόν ἐστιν ἐνίοτε κατὰ τὰς ὀξείας νόσους· ἕλκωσις γάρ τις ἐπιπολῆς ἐστιν ἡ ξυσματώδης διάθεσις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπαγωγὰς, πλάδον

And by virtue of such a power it also renders easy the conveyances of the residues from the liver, so that, when all the moistures from above are collected in the intestines, and especially in the lower ones, through their quantity 686 the sodden moistness (pladōs) arises, while through their quality the intestine is scraped. For indeed oxymel, having a detergent power, would in time scrape the intestine; and so too the bilious residue which descends along with it, having the same power; whence even without oxymel, if it should ever happen to be in excess, it scrapes the intestine. There are times, too, when this becomes harmful in acute diseases, especially because it prevents flatus from passing through, but makes it run back; and further, in another way as well, it enfeebles and chills the extremities. And this is the only harm I know of that arises through oxymel which is also worthy of being written down. For the intestine to be scraped is sometimes not good in acute diseases; for the scraping condition is a kind of superficial ulceration.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπαγωγὰς, πλάδον, γυιοῖ, ξυσματώδης διάθεσις

By a power of this kind it also makes easy the drawing-down of the residues from the liver, so that, since all the moistures from above are collected in the intestines, and especially in the lower ones, sogginess arises through their quantity 686 and the intestine is scraped through their quality. For oxymel, having a detergent power, would in time scrape the intestine; and so would the bilious residue that descends together with it, since it has the same power. Hence, even without oxymel, if it ever happens to be in excess, it scrapes the intestine. "But sometimes this proves harmful in acute diseases, above all because it prevents flatus from passing through and makes it run back; and further it also otherwise enfeebles the limbs and chills the extremities. This is the only harm I know of arising from oxymel that is also worth writing about." That the intestine should be scraped is sometimes not good in acute diseases, for the scraping condition is a kind of superficial ulceration.

K15.686-687K. XV 686–687, ch. 3.30 difficulty low (0.68/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/6 (c) pass 10/12 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

οὖσα δὲ τοιαύτη λυπεῖται πρὸς ἁπάντων 687 τῶν διεξερχομένων καὶ μάλιστα τῶν ἐχόντων τινὰ δῆξιν· λυπηθὲν δὲ τὸ ἔντερον, εἰ μὴ παραχρῆμα τὴν ἔκκρισιν τῶν δακνόντων ποιήσαιτο, πρὸς τὴν ἄνω χώραν ἀναπέμπει τὰ διεξερχόμενα. κατὰ τοῦτον οὖν τὸν λόγον καὶ ταῖς τούτου φύσαις ἄνω συμβαίνει παλινδρομεῖν. φυσικὴν γὰρ ἐνέργειαν ἔχει τὰ ἔντερα τὴν περισταλτικὴν, ὡς ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῶν φυσικῶν δυνάμεων ὑπομνήμασι δέδεικται· ταχύτητος δ᾿ ἔργον ἐστὶν ἀπωθεῖσθαι τὰ περιεχόμενα πρὸς τῶν μερῶν ἐκείνων ἐν οἶς περιέχεται. ὅταν μὲν οὖν ἄνωθεν ἡ κίνησις ἄρξηται, κάτω τὴν ἔκκρισιν ἴσχει τὰ περιεχόμενα· κάτωθεν δὲ ἀρξαμένης αὐτῆς ἄνω φέρεται. καὶ τοῦτό γε σαφῶς φαίνεται κᾀν τῷ κατὰ φύσιν ἔχειν συμβαῖνον, ὅταν ὑπιούσης ἀθρόως ποτὲ χολῆς εἰς τὰ κάτω τῶν ἐντέρων ἐπίσχωμεν τὴν ἔκκρισιν ἢ τῷ παρεῖναι τοιαύτην πρᾶξιν, ἣν μὴ διακόπτειν δυνατόν ἐστιν, ἢ τῷ μὴ παρεῖναι πλησίον ἀπόπατον· ἐν γὰρ τοῖς τοιούτοις καιροῖς ἀνατρεχούσης αἰσθανόμεθα τῆς χολῆς οὕτως ὡς μηκέτι ἐκκριθῆναι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ταχύτητος, τούτου φύσαις

And being such, it is pained by all 687 the things that pass through it, and especially by those that have some biting quality; and the intestine, once pained, if it does not immediately effect the excretion of the biting matters, sends back what is passing through toward the upper region. On this account, then, it also comes about that the flatus in it runs back upward. For the intestines have a natural activity, the peristaltic one, as has been shown in the commentaries On the Natural Faculties; and it is the work of swiftness (ταχύτητος) to thrust away the contents from those parts in which they are contained. So when the movement begins from above, the contents have their excretion downward; but when it begins from below, they are carried upward. And this is clearly seen to happen even in the natural state, when, as bile is at some time descending all at once into the lower intestines, we hold back the excretion either because some such activity is in hand which cannot be interrupted, or because there is no privy near at hand; for on such occasions we perceive the bile running back up in such a way that it is no longer excreted.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταχύτητος (possibly corrupt; ταύτης 'its work' would give better sense), ἀπωθεῖσθαι ... πρὸς τῶν μερῶν (passive 'thrust away by the parts' vs. 'away from the parts')

Being of this kind, it is distressed by all 687 the things passing through it, and especially by those that have some biting quality. And the intestine, once distressed, if it does not at once effect the excretion of the biting things, sends the things passing through back up to the upper region. By this reasoning, then, it also happens that its flatus runs back upward. For the intestines have the peristaltic as their natural activity, as has been shown in the commentaries On the Natural Faculties; and it is the work of swiftness (?) that the contents are thrust away by those parts in which they are contained. When, therefore, the motion begins from above, the contents have their excretion downward; but when it begins from below, they are carried upward. And this is clearly seen to happen even when one is in the natural state, when, bile having at some time descended all at once into the lower parts of the intestines, we hold back the excretion, either because some activity is at hand which it is not possible to interrupt, or because there is no privy nearby. For on such occasions we perceive the bile running back up, so that it is no longer excreted.

Greek (Kühn)

τινὲς μὲν 688 οὖν ἐπὶ τῇ τοιαύτῃ τῆς χολῆς ἀναδρομῇ τὸν στόμαχον ἴσχουσι κακῶς· ἔνιοι δὲ συμπληροῦνταί τε καὶ δακνωδῶς ἀλγοῦσι τὴν κεφαλὴν, ἔνιοι δὲ ἀμφότερα πάσχουσιν. οὕτως οὖν ἡ καὶ ξυσματώδης ἐν τοῖς ἐντέροις διάθεσις ἐνίοτε μὲν τὴν ἔκκρισιν εἰς τὸ κάτωθεν ἐργάζεται ταχέως, οὐχ ὑπομένουσα τὴν τῶν πασχόντων ἀνίαν, ἐνίοτε δὲ κάτωθεν πρὸς τὴν ἄνωθεν χώραν, ὅταν μὴ παραχρῆμα τὸ διεξερχόμενον ἐκπέσῃ διὰ τῆς ἔδρας, ἀλλ᾿ ὁ κατ᾿ αὐτὴν μῦς ὁ κυκλοτερῶς συσταλεὶς καὶ σφίγξας τὸ πέρας τοῦ ἀπευθυσμένου, τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆς περισταλτικῆς κινήσεως ἐκ τῶν κάτω μερῶν ἄνω ποιήσηται. διὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὖν οὐ φῦσα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ περίττωμα κατὰ τὰς τοιαύτας διαθέσεις παλινδρομεῖ. συμβαίνει δὲ καὶ τὴν δύναμιν βλάπτεσθαι διὰ τὴν ἑπομένην δῆξιν ἐν τῇ διόδῳ· τὰ γὰρ ξυόμενα, κᾂν βραχυτάτην ἴσχῃ τὸ διεξερχόμενον δῆξιν, ἀνιᾶται σφοδρῶς· ἴσμεν δὲ ὅτι πᾶν ἄλγημα καταλυτικόν ἐστι δυνάμεως καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τῶν ἀκρωτηρίων ψυκτικόν. ταύτην καὶ οἶδα μούνην βλάβην τὴν δι᾿ ὀξυμέλιτος γιγνομένην, ἥτις καὶ ἀξίη γραφῆς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ξυσματώδης, στόμαχον

Some, then, 688 upon such a running back up of the bile have their stomachos in a bad state; some become filled up and suffer a biting pain in the head; and some suffer both. Thus, then, the scraping (ξυσματώδης) condition in the intestines sometimes produces the excretion downward quickly, not enduring the distress of the parts affected, and sometimes from below toward the region above, whenever what is passing through does not fall out at once through the seat, but the muscle at it, having contracted in a circle and constricted the end of the rectum, makes the beginning of the peristaltic movement from the lower parts upward. For this reason, therefore, not only flatus but also residue runs back in such conditions. It also happens that the faculty is harmed because of the biting that follows in the passage; for the parts that are being scraped, even if what passes through has the slightest biting, are distressed violently; and we know that every pain is dissolvent of the faculty and for this reason also chilling to the extremities. And this is the only harm I know of that arises from oxymel, which is also worth writing about.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ξυσματώδης, στόμαχον, συμπληροῦνται, ποιήσηται

Some, 688 then, in consequence of such a running back of the bile have their stomach in a bad state; others are filled up and suffer a biting pain in the head; and some suffer both. In the same way, then, the scraping condition in the intestines sometimes quickly brings about excretion downwards, not enduring the distress of the affected parts, and sometimes from below towards the upper region, when what is passing through is not immediately expelled through the seat, but the muscle at that place, contracting circularly and constricting the end of the rectum, makes the beginning of the peristaltic motion from the lower parts upwards. For this reason, then, not only wind but also residue runs back in such conditions. It also happens that the strength is harmed on account of the biting that ensues in the passage; for parts that are being scraped, even if what passes through has the very slightest biting quality, are vehemently distressed. And we know that every pain is dissolutive of the strength, and for this reason also cooling to the extremities. 'This is also the only harm I know that comes about through oxymel which is also worth writing about.'

Τὴν δύναμιν ἣν ἔχει διελθὼν, ἐπὶ τὸν τοῦ καιροῦ λόγον, ἐν ᾧ μάλιστ᾿ ἄν τις αὐτὸ προσφέροιτο, νῦν ἀφίκετο, πότε μάλιστ᾿ ἄν τις ὀξυμέλιτι χρῷτο τῶν ἐπὶ ῥοφήμασι διαιτωμένων· ἐφεξῆς δ᾿ εἰ καὶ μὴ ῥοφήμασιν, ἀλλ᾿ ἐπὶ τῷ ποτῷ μόνον διαιτώμενός τις εἴη καὶ τὸν ἐπ᾿ ἐκείνοις καιρὸν 690 διδάσκει. φησὶν οὖν ἐπὶ τῶν ῥοφήματι χρωμένων ἐκεῖνον μόνον φυλάττεσθαι δεῖν τὸν καιρὸν, ἐν ᾦ ῥόφημα δεῖ προσφέρεσθαι. πτισάνης δὲ δηλονότι χυλός ἐστι ῥόφημα χά ριν τοῦ θρέψαι τὸ σῶμα καὶ τὴν δύναμιν ῥῶσαι λαμβανόμενος· ἔστι δὲ ἐς ταῦτα καλὸς, εἰ μόνος ἐν τῇ γαστρὶ περιέχοιτο κατὰ τὸν τῆς θρέψεως καιρόν· ἔμαθες γὰρ ἔμπροσθεν ὡς ἡ ποικιλία τῶν προσφερομένων, καὶ μάλισθ᾿ ὅταν ἀνόμοια ταῖς δυνάμεσιν εἴη, ταραχὴν ἐργάζεται κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα. καὶ διὰ τοῦτ᾿ οὖν ἄχρι πεφθῆναι τὸν χυλὸν, οὐ χρὴ πίνειν ὀξύμελι· πεφθέντος δὲ ἔξεστι καὶ διὰ τῆς νυκτὸς λαμβάνειν καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἐπιοῦσαν ἡμέραν πρὸ τοῦ ῥοφήματος· ἀρκεῖ δὲ τὸν μεταξὺ χρόνον τοῦ ῥοφήματος καὶ τοῦ ὀξυμέλιτος, ὥστε εἰς κενήν τε ἅμα καὶ καθαρὰν ἐκ τοῦ προδιασμήχεσθαι τὴν γαστέρα, τὸν χυλὸν τῆς πτισάνης ἐμπεσεῖν.
